Badges Quick Win

Silhouette — the daily badge guessing game

Details

One badge per day is shown as a black silhouette (and a second difficulty: heavily blurred) and the player types or picks the set name; a correct first guess pays XP scaled by the badge's TBRI. Streaks and a wordle-style shareable emoji-free result grid. Why it fits: the catalog is the answer key, and rarity is the natural difficulty dial — obscure badges become hard puzzles for free. It slots into the existing 13-game, achievement and XP machinery instead of inventing a new economy. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: silhouettes of badges with dark artwork are unreadable on dark theme; the image treatment needs a per-badge contrast check and a "skip" that does not break the streak unfairly. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-02.md

Design Quick Win

Contrast and colour-independence for rarity tiers and charts

Details

rarity is signalled by colour plus text, but the distribution bars, heatmap cells and the six-tier chips encode tier by hue alone, and light-theme `.light` tokens were never contrast-checked against every tier. Add a low-variant pattern/texture fill and a text/symbol label per tier, and raise any tier/token pair below AA 4.5:1 (3:1 for large/graphic) to pass. Effort: M. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-07.md

Badges Quick Win

Badge art revision detector

Details

At each sync, hash a downsampled copy of every badge image per set_id and version and store the hash; when the hash changes while the version does not, mark the badge 'art revised' and show an old-versus-new side by side on the detail page with the date it happened. The catalog gains an 'art revised' filter and the change writes its own changelog row. Uses the existing Helix image URLs plus one stored hash column, no new provider needed. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: CDN re-encodes and resize variants change bytes without a real art change, so hashing the downsampled pixels rather than raw bytes is mandatory or the alert becomes permanent noise. Why: Twitch quietly reissues badge art under the same set and version, and the catalog currently has no idea it happened. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Badge art alt-text generation

Details

Generate a short factual description of each badge's artwork (dominant shapes, colours, any text inside the art) and store it as the alt text used by the grid tile, the detail image and the OG card, with an admin override per badge. Runs as a pass over newly added badges in the sync. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a confident wrong description ('a red heart') is worse for screen-reader users than a neutral fallback, so anything ungenerated or low-confidence must fall back to 'badge titled X'. Why: The catalog is a wall of images with no words, and screen-reader users meet the wall first. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Category contradiction flagger

Details

Derive a probable category from each badge's set_id and name patterns, then flag rows whose provider category contradicts the pattern ('set_id says Subscriber, provider says Prime Gaming') in an admin queue with a one-click accept and a rule-version history. Recurring contradictions become rules rather than one-off fixes. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: rules overfit one provider's naming and mislabel genuine variants, so every suggestion must be reviewed and the rule set versioned with an undo. Why: Category is the catalog's main browse axis and it is provider-supplied and demonstrably inconsistent. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Paid-badge cost archive

Details

A curated table records what the badges that required real money cost at release (charity badges, Turbo, paid campaigns) with their currency and region variants, and shows a cost line on the badge page beside its free or paid status, plus a catalog filter and a total for a category. Prices come from the admin table and provider notes, never invented. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: mixing currencies and regions into one number produces a confident wrong figure, so each price must carry its currency, region and source date, or it must not be shown. Why: A meaningful slice of the catalog cost real money and the catalog records only the word paid. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Structured catalog query language

Details

A documented predicate grammar over the catalog: cat:twitchcon year:2024 owners:<500 window:open -owned tier:legendary, parsed server-side into parameterized PostgREST filters, with every filter state exposing a copy-as-query button and a shareable URL, and named queries a member can save and reopen. A short syntax reference sits under the search box. It filters the catalog and is deliberately separate from the matching-based search (alias, cross-script, text-in-art), which answers a different question. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a hand-rolled parser over user input is an injection and denial-of-service surface if it reaches SQL unchecked, so fields and operators must be allow-listed and only parameterized filters emitted. Why: The catalog holds the answer to thousands of precise questions and a predicate language is the only interface that can ask them. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Design Quick Win

High-contrast variant driven by prefers-contrast

Details

The site ships dark and light and is fixing light hairlines, but nothing responds to a visitor who has asked the operating system for more contrast. Add a third token block, selected by prefers-contrast: more and also offered as an explicit theme option, that lifts body copy to an AAA ratio, promotes --line to a solid colour-mixed hairline, and removes the translucent surfaces and backdrop blurs that reduce legibility over the ambient gradient. It is a token override on the html element, so no component changes and useChartTheme inherits it. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a variant tested only on the catalog that breaks the charts, whose palette is read from the same tokens, or a variant so flat it stops being recognisably this site. Why: Contrast is a preference people state to their OS, and a site this visually busy owes them a version that respects it. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Native control and OS chrome theming pass

Details

Native widgets ignore the design system: form controls can render in the OS light style inside a dark page, and while the scrollbar is themed, colour-scheme is never declared so ::selection, the caret, the placeholder, autofill, the date picker and the file button are all left to the browser. One pass declares color-scheme per theme, themes ::selection, ::placeholder and caret-color, styles the checkbox, radio and range tokens, and gives autofill a background that does not flash yellow over the dark surface. Scoped entirely to globals.css. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a control that looks correct in dark and unreadable in light, or an autofill override that removes an affordance the browser uses for security. Why: The seams where a design system meets the browser are exactly where a polished site starts to look assembled rather than designed. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Queued toast layer for action feedback

Details

Transient confirmations, copied, claimed, synced, wishlisted, settings saved, insufficient coins, currently land wherever the component happens to put them and some land nowhere. Add a toast layer with a small queue: single-line, bottom-anchored on desktop and above the mobile action bar on phones, a variant per semantic (neutral, success, warning, danger), at most three stacked with older ones retiring, a dismissal after a token duration plus a manual close, and instant appearance under reduced motion. It reads no data, it is purely the presentation of outcomes. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: toasts stacking over the game board and covering the controls, a queue that buries an error behind three copied messages, or a toast announced twice by a screen reader alongside the live region. Why: The site is dense with small actions, and consistent acknowledgement is what makes them feel like they landed. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Design family for empty and zero states

Details

The app renders empty states rather than crashing, but they are ad hoc strings: an empty inventory, a showcase with nothing in it, a wishlist, a category with no badges, a leaderboard in a quiet period, a search with no matches. Define one state family with a shared composition, a small inline SVG in the badge language, one sentence saying what this place is, and exactly one primary action (sync your badges, browse the catalog, add a badge), plus a secondary why-is-this-empty line that distinguishes empty-because-of-you from empty-because-of-the-schedule. Every empty state is one of the defined variants and the variant is checked in review. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a charming empty state that masks a genuine failure, so an empty inventory caused by a provider error reads identically to an inventory that is truly empty. Why: A database site is empty for a dozen different reasons and the visitor deserves to know which one they are looking at. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Lifecycle glyph set

Details

Upcoming, open, closing-soon, closed, expired and extinct are each a text pill, so scanning a table means reading every row. Design a small closed set of lifecycle glyphs on the shared icon grid, a clock for upcoming, an open bracket for live, a notched bracket for closing soon, a closed bracket for ended and a struck mark for extinct, each paired with its label and its data-semantic colour and used in the status pill, the table, the tile corner and the deadline calendar. Glyph always travels with its label wherever a screen reader reads it. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: glyphs differing only in a detail that vanishes at 14 px, or an extinct glyph that reads as an error state rather than a neutral archive. Why: Whether a badge can still be claimed is the first question on every screen here, and it should be answerable by shape. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Profil Quick Win

Opt-in Collector Directory

Details

A browsable opt-in directory of members with filters over verified style, level band, category strength, region and last activity, each row a compact card linking to the profile. It is a discovery surface and not a ranking: there is no strength ordering, default sort is alphabetical or by join ordinal, and each field is opted in separately so a level can be visible while a region is not. It also gives races, relays and pools a place to find a partner. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it becomes a de facto leaderboard or a scrape target, so the default sort must not be by strength and filtering needs rate limits. Why: The site has thousands of members and no way to find one of them, which makes every social feature above harder to use. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Performance Quick Win

A hard timeout on every catalog read

Details

Every query in src/lib/queries.ts goes out with no abort signal, so one slow Supabase pooler response holds the whole server render until the platform kills it, producing a 504 instead of the empty state the .catch() wrappers were written for. Pass AbortSignal.timeout on the PostgREST reads (about 2500 ms, from a setting) and let the existing fallbacks render the empty state. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: too tight a limit turns a merely busy database into a site that looks empty, so log every abort with its label so the rate is visible before the value is tuned. Why: A pooler hiccup should degrade the catalog to an empty state with a logged cause, not hand every visitor a gateway timeout. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Bound the cacheable catalog URLs

Details

/badges is ISR with revalidate=120 while every filter lives in searchParams, so each combination of q, category, rarity, sort, price and page is its own cache entry, and a crawler or a shared link with arbitrary parameters mints unbounded entries, each one a fresh PostgREST read. Canonicalise the parameter set, redirecting unknown keys away while preserving the ones BadgeExplorer reads, and treat the free-text search variant as a short-cache or no-store route. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: dropping a parameter a user legitimately shares breaks their link, so recognise only the keys the explorer actually reads and redirect the rest rather than rejecting the URL. Why: An unbounded cache key space silently turns the CDN from a shield into a fan-out amplifier for the database. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Structured breadcrumbs on every nested route

Details

Only /badges/[slug] renders a breadcrumb, and it is hand-written inside the page. Add one shared Breadcrumbs component fed by a small route-to-parent manifest, render it on every nested page (blog post, profile, game, compare, admin tab) and emit the matching BreadcrumbList JSON-LD from that same manifest, so the visible trail and the structured data cannot disagree. The final crumb is the current page, is not a link, and the component takes the locale-aware Link. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a manifest that drifts from the real routes produces broken parent links, which is worse than having no breadcrumb. Why: A site with four parallel catalog sections and a dozen sibling pages needs a visible position indicator, and one manifest keeps it honest. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Contextual empty states with recovery actions

Details

Every empty state today is one muted sentence such as empty, no stats or the shared setup hint, so a visitor who lands on one has nowhere to go. Give each the actual next step: a zero-result catalog query shows the three nearest badge titles by a cheap similarity match plus one-click links that drop just the rarity filter or just the category filter, built from the current query string; an empty inventory puts the sync button inside the card; an empty leaderboard links to the catalog. Keep the raw setup hint only for the genuinely un-migrated database case. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: fuzzy suggestions on a nonsense query present irrelevant badges as if they matched, so they must be labelled as suggestions and capped at three. Why: The moment a search returns nothing is the moment a first-time visitor leaves, and a recovery action is the cheapest way to keep them. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Catalog-only mode for non-gamers

Details

Someone who came to look up a badge meets XP, coins, a wheel, heists and thirteen games in the same header and home page. Add a catalog-only preference (a cookie plus an optional profile field) that hides the games, wheel, feed, steal and compare entries and the level and coin HUD, leaving the catalog, blog, changelog, FAQ and stats, with a one-click switch back in the account menu. Reuse the existing feature flags from site settings so the same switches that disable a feature server-side shape the reduced nav. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: two header variants to test forever, and a member who turns it on cannot find the switch back and concludes the games were removed. Why: A large share of badge-site traffic wants a reference table, not an economy, and hiding the economy makes the reference table legible. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Highlight and explain search matches

Details

The catalog predicate matches a query against the badge title, the set id and the category, but a result row renders only the title, so a badge returned for a category or set-id match looks like a wrong result. Return the matched field and the matched substring from the list query, emphasise the substring inside the title with a marked-up span, and when the match was not the title render a small line naming where it matched (category Sub, set id 1234). Submit the query with the same accent styling and keep the highlight markup out of the accessible name so screen readers hear the plain title. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: echoing back unescaped user input into the DOM is an injection point, and a highlight computed with a different case-folding rule than the database's ilike marks the wrong characters. Why: A search result that cannot explain why it is there reads as a broken search, which is how people stop trusting the catalog. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

ARIA combobox search with suggestions

Details

Catalog search is a plain GET form with no suggestions. Upgrade the input to the combobox pattern with role set, an expanded state and an active-descendant over an options list of the top six matches, each with its image, title and status chip, while keeping the surrounding form a real GET so search still works with JavaScript disabled. Reuse the existing listBadges predicate with a debounce and a stale-response guard. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: this pattern is easy to get wrong (the language switcher already had to move the active descendant onto the listbox owner) and a laggy suggestion query makes typing feel broken. Why: Typing three letters and seeing the badge you meant is the fastest path into a 476-row catalog. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Unsaved-changes guard on the editors

Details

The profile customizer carries thirty-five settings and the admin settings panel more, and both save only on an explicit button, so a stray back gesture or tab click silently discards everything. Track dirty state, show an unsaved chip beside the save button, intercept in-app link navigation and the unload event with a confirm, and pair it with a per-group reset. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a guard that fires on a no-op such as opening the colour picker and closing it trains people to dismiss it, and a guard on the admin panel can block an urgent change. Why: Losing a carefully built profile theme to a mis-tapped back button is a permanent reason not to bother customising again. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Localised error taxonomy with a cooldown timer

Details

Game and economy failures render whatever string arrives in the error field, so a member sees Round failed or Network error in English inside an otherwise translated page. Map the known server codes (rate limited, insufficient coins, feature disabled, maintenance, session expired) to localised messages with a try-again-in countdown for the one-per-second limit and a concrete action link for each, announced politely. Keep an unmapped-code fallback that shows a generic message plus a reference instead of the raw string. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: an unmapped code leaks a server internal, and a cooldown that counts down while the server still rejects teaches people to distrust it. Why: A rate limit is a normal part of playing thirteen games, and today it is explained in the wrong language and with no remedy. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Route progress indicator

Details

Clicking a nav link or a filter chip gives no feedback until Next finishes streaming, so a slow navigation feels like an ignored click. Add a slim indeterminate progress bar driven by the pending transition the filter bar already computes and by the router's link status, with a delay threshold so cached navigations never flash it, positioned under the sticky header so it reads as chrome rather than content. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a bar that appears and vanishes in thirty milliseconds is visual noise, and one that never completes on a streamed page reads as a stuck site. Why: Perceived responsiveness on a catalog that re-queries on every filter click is worth a few lines of client code. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Print and reading view

Details

Someone who wants a badge's window, how-to-earn and source on paper currently prints the whole site chrome, and the countdowns print as dashes because they are client timers. Add a print stylesheet and a reading-mode variant that strip navigation and interactive widgets, expand collapsed FAQ answers, swap relative timers for absolute instants, and add a footer with the data source and the generation timestamp. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a print stylesheet that drifts from the DOM prints empty cards, and printing a live timer is worse than useless, so timers must be replaced rather than left running. Why: A badge deadline is exactly the kind of fact people want to keep, and the site currently offers no way to take it away. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Touch target and hit-slop minimums

Details

The filter chips, the badge-tile secondary controls, the countdown units and several icon-only buttons render well under the recommended minimum touch size on a phone. Introduce a shared tap-minimum token applied through a pseudo-element hit slop on interactive chips and icon buttons so the visual size stays but the target grows, and require an accessible name on any icon-only control in review. Audit the header, filter bar, pagination and the games' bet controls against it. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: larger invisible targets overlap and steal each other's taps on a six-column grid, which is worse than a small target. Why: Most badge lookups happen on a phone, where a 24 px chip is a coin toss between two filters. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Mobile input zoom and keyboard hints

Details

Because the type scale runs small, iOS Safari zooms the viewport whenever the search box, the bet input or the bio textarea is focused, and every field gets the same text keyboard. Set a sixteen-pixel minimum font on form controls and give each field the right input mode, enter-key hint, autocomplete and capitalisation behaviour, numeric for the bet and search for the search box. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: raising control text to sixteen pixels makes the compact filters look inconsistent, and a numeric input mode can block pasting a formatted number. Why: The zoom-on-focus jump is the single most visible mobile defect on a site whose primary control is a small search box. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Long-press tile shortcut menu

Details

Right-clicking or long-pressing a badge tile does nothing. Add one shortcut menu on the tile, shared by pointer right-click and touch long-press, offering the navigational actions that otherwise need a round trip: open the detail page, add to the compare selection, filter the grid to this badge's category, and show the rest of this badge's set. Make the same menu reachable from a visible overflow button so keyboard users get it too. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a custom context menu breaks text selection and the browser menu people expect, and a long-press that fires during a scroll opens the menu on every flick. Why: The catalog is 476 tiles deep and the fast paths between them should not require a full page load. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Per-section failure isolation with retry

Details

The home page asks for six things and on any error renders one setup hint for the lot, and a section that throws blanks its whole column. Wrap each section in its own error boundary so a failing query shows a small could-not-load card with a retry that re-runs only that boundary, and the page degrades instead of emptying. Keep one quiet summary line so the page does not become a wall of error cards. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: six error cards read as a broken site, and a retry that re-throws immediately without changing its message teaches people it does nothing. Why: One slow provider currently erases the whole landing page, which is the worst possible first impression. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Forced-colors and High Contrast support

Details

Forced-colors mode strips background colours, and the tier chips, live dots, rarity bars and the wheel indicate state with background colour alone, so they collapse to unreadable blocks in Windows High Contrast. Add forced-colors rules that restore visible borders through the system colour keywords, and make sure every state also carries a text or border signal rather than colour alone. Test in a real forced-colors environment rather than a CSS toggle. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: forced-colors rules that fight the theme tokens produce double borders in normal mode, and a drawn wheel needs its own non-colour fallback rather than a stylesheet rule. Why: High Contrast mode is a common accessibility setting for low-vision users, and rarity is the site's central encoded fact. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Chart text alternatives and keyboard tooltips

Details

The owner-trend chart and the stats dashboard's trend, donut and histogram are visuals whose data is reachable only by hovering. Give each chart a described-by sentence generated from the series, plus a visually hidden data table of a sampled series, and make the tooltip reachable with arrow keys so a keyboard user reads the same points. Sample the series for long ranges so the hidden table stays small. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: hidden tables on a thousand-point dashboard bloat the DOM, and a generated sentence that misstates the direction of a noisy series is a wrong fact stated confidently. Why: The owner curve is the evidence behind every rarity claim, and right now it exists only as pixels. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Details

Content Quick Win

IndexNow ping on publish

Details

When a post, glossary entry, tutorial or refreshed badge page is published, the publish path submits the affected URLs to IndexNow and warms those routes' cache, so new content is discovered in hours instead of at the next sitemap crawl. The publish action already knows the exact slugs it touched, so this is one outbound request from the existing route handler, deduplicated by URL and capped per run, with failures logged as a heartbeat rather than blocking the publish. Data: the slugs written by the publish action plus the canonical host. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a loop or a bulk sync submits thousands of URLs and the endpoint throttles the host; cap per-run submissions and never ping from a catalog sync, only from an editorial write. Why: Editorial content is only worth writing if it is indexed while the drop it describes is still live. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Performance Quick Win

Mirror the badge art into our storage

Details

Every badge tile loads its image from a third-party CDN this project does not control, so a rate limit or a removed file there is a hole in the catalog with no fallback. At sync time, copy the 1x, 2x and 4x PNGs whose URLs badges already stores into a Supabase Storage bucket (roughly 1,400 small files) and serve them with a long cache header, keeping the original URL in the row as a fallback. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a bucket that is not served through the same image path as today is worse than the CDN, so keep the original URL as the fallback until the copy is verified for every row. Why: The catalog's whole visual identity depends on another company's CDN staying up and staying unmetered. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Parallelise the potat page fetches

Details

fetchAllDistribution and fetchAllOwners walk their cursors one page at a time, so the sync's wall clock is the sum of every page's latency and sits close to the serverless limit for no reason other than sequencing. Fetch with a small bounded window (three or four in flight) while still honouring the Retry-After: 60 the provider returns. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: parallel pages against a provider that rate-limits by request count turn one sync into a 429 storm, so keep the pool small, back the whole pool off on the first Retry-After and expose the window as a setting. Why: The owner sweep is the sync most likely to time out, and its time is currently the sum of hundreds of sequential round trips. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Badges Quick Win

Collection Completion Solver

Details

Given a user's inventory, the currently claimable badges and the XP curve, the solver computes the cheapest concrete path to the next milestone: which badges to claim, which games to play and in what order to reach a chosen target (next level, next achievement, tier completion), with the coin/XP cost of each route and a "no feasible path" verdict when there isn't one. Why it fits: Inventory, XP curve, achievement predicates and game rewards all live server-side already; nobody has joined them into a single planner. It converts the site's many separate progress bars into one actionable list. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: Optimization over a live, changing catalog produces a plan that is stale before it is read — it must recompute against fresh data and label its assumptions, not cache a route for a day. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Claimable-now filter with time-remaining sort

Details

A top-level filter chip on `/badges` that shows only badges whose claim window is currently open, sorted by "closes soonest" by default, with the remaining time rendered as the existing `.countdown` component on each tile. Badges Quick Win

Badge name collision pages

Details

Detect catalog rows that share a display title or a near-identical name, and publish a disambiguation page listing them with the distinguishing facts (set_id, category, window, owner count) plus cross-links from each badge page. The search result for an ambiguous query routes to that page instead of picking one arbitrarily. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: an over-eager similarity threshold creates disambiguation pages for badges that are already distinct, so collisions need an exact-title trigger first and fuzzy matching only as a suggestion. Why: Twitch reuses names like TwitchCon and Prime repeatedly, and the catalog resolves them to whichever row sorted first. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Localized badge metadata layer

Details

Add a translation table keyed on badge id and on provider category terms, holding name, short description and category label per locale, with fallback to the provider's English string when a translation is missing. The badge page, catalog facets, OG card and feeds all read through it, and the admin view shows per-locale fill so an untranslated row is visibly untranslated rather than silently English. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: machine-translating badge names produces plausible nonsense that then outranks the correct English in search, so every term must carry a human or machine marker and machine output must not be indexed as canonical. Why: Eleven locales of interface chrome around English badge names is only half a localized catalog. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badge rename tracker

Writes a changelog row per rename. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: matching names across syncs by list position rather than by id invents a rename every time a provider reorders its output, so matching must be id-first. Badges Quick Win

Timezone fairness index per window

Details

Score every claim window by how much of the world was awake at its open and close: weight the window's hours against a population-by-timezone curve and produce an index plus a plain label such as closes at 03:00 for Europe. Window pages and the drop list can sort by it, and the catalog can call out windows that only ever opened during one hemisphere's daytime. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: the population curve is an approximation and the score can read as a fairness verdict on Twitch, so present it as a planning aid and always show the underlying hour table so anyone can check it. Why: A large part of the audience loses drops to the clock and a window-level score is the only way to see that pattern in the data. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Community ownership share

Details

For every badge, compute the share of this site's synced members who own it, show it on the detail page beside the global owner count, and use it as a catalog sort. It is a second, independent rarity signal that still works where potat returns nothing, and it can be labelled honestly as a lower bound ('at least N of our members hold this'). Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the member base is small and self-selected, so the share must be presented as site-relative with its denominator visible and never as a claim about global ownership. Why: A second ownership signal is what makes the site's rarity numbers defensible when one provider goes quiet. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Content Quick Win

Per-badge generated FAQ

Details

Each badge detail page gains a small FAQ block built from its own row: when the claim window closes, whether it is still obtainable, how many owners it has, which category it belongs to, and whether it is free or paid — each answer short and linking to the tool that owns the fact. It renders as FAQPage structured data so search engines can lift exact answers, and it is generated at render time from live columns rather than stored prose, so answers expire with the data. Data: badge row (window, category, paid flag), latest owner count, TBRI tier. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a generated answer restates a null or stale column and produces confidently wrong schema; suppress any question whose underlying field is missing instead of writing a hedge. Why: It makes each of the 476 badge pages answer the exact long-tail question a searcher typed, with no editorial effort per badge. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Quick Win

Curated themed badge lists

Details

Hand-curated, editorially framed lists that cut across categories — first badges to chase as a newcomer, badges worth a TwitchCon trip, the finest pixel-art badges in the catalog — stored as an ordered list of badge IDs with a rationale per entry and an intro paragraph. Unlike a generated landing page the value is the selection and the sentence explaining why each badge made it, and each entry links to the badge while showing its live tier and owner count. Lists are versioned so a curator can replace an expired entry with a note. Data: an ordered badge-ID list, the badge rows for live state, authored copy. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a list goes stale as its badges expire and becomes a page of dead ends; drive a re-check from badge expiry and hide an expired entry rather than leaving it in place. Why: Curation is the one thing a database cannot generate, and it is what gives a list page a reason to exist beside the filter UI. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Quick Win

Explain-this-number tooltips

Details

Wherever the site shows jargon or a computed number — TBRI, tier name, owner count, momentum, half-life, wear share — the label carries a focusable tooltip with a one-sentence plain-language definition and a link to the full methodology page. Definitions come from one shared registry keyed by term so the same word means the same thing on a badge tile, a leaderboard and a chart legend, and keyboard focus opens it without a mouse. Data: a definitions map in code, linked to the glossary and methodology content. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: tooltips multiply until every label has a popover and reading the page becomes an exercise in hovering; restrict it to the defined vocabulary list and never wrap ordinary words. Why: The site's best features are its numbers, and every undefined number is a reader leaving to look up what TBRI means. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Design Quick Win

Quick-look split pane on wide screens

Details

On viewports wide enough for two columns the catalog becomes master-detail: the BadgeGrid on one side and a pane on the other showing the focused badge's art at 4x, its rarity, owner count, window and a link to the full page, filled as focus or hover moves through the grid without any navigation. The pane reads the same BadgeRow the tile already has, so it costs no extra query, and below the breakpoint the layout falls back to the current single column. It composes with the roving-tabindex grid work already on the board rather than replacing it. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: hover-driven preview on touch devices where there is no hover, or a pane that swallows the click and makes opening a badge ambiguous. Why: Browsing 476 badges is a scanning task, and inspecting without losing your place in the list is the whole game of a catalog. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Per-breakpoint information budget on badge tiles

Details

A badge tile carries name, set, owner count, tier chip and countdown, and at two columns the text wraps to four lines while at six it truncates to nothing useful. Define an explicit budget per breakpoint, two facts at 2 columns (name and tier), three at 4, five at 6, using named slots inside .badge-tile so a tile never renders a field its width cannot hold and the omitted fields are absent from the DOM rather than clipped by overflow. The rule lives once in the tile component, not per page. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: tiles that clip mid-word, or a mobile tile that hides the only fact that matters, whether the badge is still claimable. Why: The grid is the site's primary surface and it should read cleanly at every width instead of degrading into truncation. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Scroll-adaptive compact header

Details

The header is a fixed-height sticky bar carrying brand, nav, locale switcher, theme toggle and the personal HUD, and on a phone that vertical cost is paid on every screenful. Give it two defined states: expanded at scroll top, compact after the first screen where it keeps the brand, the coin and level HUD and the account control and folds the nav into the existing sheet, transitioning over a token duration rather than a bounce. The switch is driven by scroll position with a hysteresis threshold so it cannot flicker at the boundary, and the desktop nav is exempt. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a header that jitters across the threshold, or one that hides navigation on a desktop where it should stay put. Why: Persistent chrome should cost the reader less the deeper they read, not the same amount on every screen. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Interaction-state tokens and a state matrix

Details

Hover, active, focus-visible, disabled, loading and selected are hand-tuned per component today, so .btn, .chip and .card-interactive each invent their own mix and a chip and a button feel subtly different in the same state. Introduce one token per role (--state-hover-bg, --state-active-scale, --state-focus-ring, --state-disabled-opacity, --state-selected-bg) and one focus-visible rule drawing a 2 px accent ring with an offset that reads on both the page background and inside a card, then document the matrix in the styleguide. This is a different axis from the elevation and radius token idea already on the board, which scales shadows and corners, not states. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a focus ring invisible against the accent-filled primary button, or a hover token leaking onto display-only chips until they look clickable. Why: A visitor touches these states hundreds of times a session, and inconsistency between a chip and a button is the difference between a system and a pile of parts. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Ambient theme bound to a live event window

Details

The catalog knows when a global event is live, a badge set with a shared short window, a TwitchCon drop, a Prime batch, from badge_events and the set metadata. For the duration of such a window, switch the site's ambient accent and --ambient wash to a palette derived from the dominant colours of that event's badge art, time-boxed to the window and reverting automatically, with a small live marker explaining why the site changed. It is a token override scoped to the html element while the window is open, not a saved preference like the existing profile palettes. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: event art yielding an unreadable accent, a theme that sticks after the window closes because the sweep is stale, or contrast regressions in charts that read the accent token. Why: The site's whole subject is timed drops, so letting the interface change during one is the design equivalent of live coverage. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Async action state machine for buttons

Details

Buttons that trigger server work, sync inventory, claim the daily bonus, copy the badge embed, submit a report, sometimes show a spinner and sometimes show nothing, and a failed action often only reaches the console. Standardise the client lifecycle as one hook plus one button presentation: idle, pending with an inline progress that replaces the label without changing the button width, success with a brief tick then back, and error where the label becomes the retry and the reason goes to a title and a toast. Preserving width through the state change is the point. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a success tick that fires before the server confirms, or a disabled button that never re-enables after a thrown error. Why: Nearly every meaningful action here is a network round trip, and the interface should say so in one consistent voice. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Designed system pages as one family

Details

404, 500, offline and maintenance are currently whatever Next renders plus the feature-flag gate. Design them as one family sharing a centred measure and a badge-flavoured conceit, a placeholder tile carrying the site's own mark reading this badge is not in the catalog, with the locale-aware message, a primary way back, a secondary retry, and a maintenance variant that shows the last sync heartbeat time so the page admits it is temporary and states how current the data is. The offline variant is served by the existing service worker for cached routes with a clear showing-what-we-have framing. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an error page that leaks a stack trace or a table name, or an offline page that presents cached catalog data as current. Why: The pages a visitor sees when something is wrong are the ones they remember, and right now they are unbranded. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Living styleguide route for the design system

Details

globals.css carries hundreds of component classes and tokens whose only documentation is the CSS itself. Add an internal styleguide route that renders every component class in every state (default, hover, focus-visible, disabled, selected, error, empty, loading), in both themes, at a wide and a narrow viewport and in both directions, each block labelled with its class name and the tokens it consumes, generated from a manifest that is checked against the stylesheet so a class with no entry fails. It becomes the review surface for the rest of these design ideas and the source for visual snapshots. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a styleguide that re-implements components instead of rendering the real classes and therefore drifts, or one shipped publicly and indexed without a noindex. Why: A design system with no place to look at itself is a set of files nobody trusts, and this site's CSS is already past that size. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Bottom action bar on badge pages for phones

Details

On a phone a badge page's primary action, the claim link, or the wishlist toggle for an upcoming badge, scrolls away immediately and the visitor has to hunt back up. Add a bottom action bar that appears once the in-flow action leaves the viewport, carrying the primary action plus one context action, wishlist or share, respecting the bottom safe-area inset and sitting above the toast layer, with the in-flow control remaining the source in the accessibility tree and the bar a shortcut to it. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: two competing primary buttons on screen at once, a bar covering the last row of content, or a bar lingering on a finished badge whose only action is gone. Why: During a live drop the visitor is holding a phone in one hand, and the one thing they came to tap should never be behind a scroll. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Icon grid and stroke contract

Details

Icons are inline SVG written per component, so the same concept is drawn at three sizes with different stroke weights and optical alignment, and nav glyphs sit on different baselines. Establish one icon contract: a 24-unit grid with a defined live area, three sizes (16, 20, 24) scaled from a single drawing, a stroke token using currentColor with one weight per size, rounded caps, and an alignment rule so glyphs in a row share a baseline, then audit the existing set into it and add a check that a new icon matches the contract. No icon font and no change of technique required. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: redrawing everything for uniformity and losing the specific glyphs that worked, or a 16 px variant that turns a detailed icon into a smudge. Why: Icons are the only ornament a dark data-dense UI can afford, and three weights of the same symbol is three visual languages. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Add-ons Quick Win

Level-gated game unlocks

Details

The high-variance games (blackjack, roulette, coin-flip ladder) stay locked until a member reaches a configured level, and each locked tile shows the level required and a one-line reason to learn the slower games first. Gates come from the admin settings document, so the panel can move or drop a gate without a deploy, and a locked tile still links to the rules page while tracking stays open. Crossing a threshold writes a small 'new game unlocked' notification. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: gating a game players already spent coins on reads as punishment, so gates must apply only to accounts created after the feature ships or be announced ahead with a grace period. Why: New members meet 13 games at once with no ordering, and a staged unlock gives the arcade a shape without removing any game. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Profil Quick Win

Showcase Provenance Captions

Details

Each of the six showcase slots gets an optional caption (about 90 characters) where the member records how the badge was earned — 'TwitchCon 2019 booth', 'gifted by @name', 'subbed in the final hour'. Captions live as a bounded array inside the existing customization document, render under the tile on the profile and inside the profile OG card, and are saved through the same customizer route. Visitors stop seeing six unexplained icons; the server length-checks and stores the text so no markup travels with it. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: captions become unmoderated free text carrying links or slurs, and the customization blob grows past its 4 KB cap. Why: It turns a showcase of images into a set of small personal histories, which is exactly the storytelling the catalog alone cannot provide. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Verified Collecting Style

Details

From the synced inventory a member is classified into one style — completionist, rarity hunter, event chaser, set loyalist, newcomer — by simple thresholds over category concentration, TBRI skew and the share of limited-window badges, recomputed monthly by the existing sync. The style is a chip on the profile with a 'why' tooltip naming the two numbers that decided it, it is a filter in the new collector directory, and races and relays can be scoped to one style. It is a deterministic classification of a collection, never a horoscope: the same inputs always give the same label, and a member can dispute it once per period. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: lazy thresholds label almost everyone a rarity hunter, or the chip shames casual collectors and quietly becomes a ranking. Why: A verified collecting style gives the social features something concrete to match on beyond level and raw rarity. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Collector Ordinal Numbers

Details

Every member gets an immutable ordinal from join order ('#142 of 8,913'), assigned by a database sequence at first profile creation and shown next to the handle on the profile, in compare and in the directory. A founders board lists the first 500 by join date, and the ordinal doubles as a stable numeric permalink that survives a handle change. It is an honest identity fact the site can compute exactly once. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: backfilling from created_at collides under concurrent writes, so a sequence — not a row count — must mint the number. Why: A permanent membership number is the simplest durable identity a community site can offer, and it makes the earliest members legible. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Performance Quick Win

Feed the filter bar from the categories view

Details

getCategories() in src/lib/queries.ts selects the category column of every row in badges and de-duplicates in JS, and BadgeExplorer calls it on every render of /badges, /active, /upcoming and /expired in all 11 locales. The stats_catalog_categories view (migration 0013) already returns one row per category with its count and is read by getSiteStats, so the filter bar should read that view instead, turning a roughly 476-row transfer into a handful of rows. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: the view needs the same anon grant as the other stats_* views, or the filter bar renders empty for logged-out visitors while logged-in staff still see it. Why: The catalog filter bar is on every status page in every language, and it currently reads the whole table to show a dropdown of about ten values. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Ship only the columns the grid renders

Details

listBadges() selects * from badges, so each of up to 96 tiles on /badges carries description, how_to_earn, click_url, removed_at and all three image URLs even though a tile renders only title, one image, rarity tier, status and owner count. Introduce a picked column list for list surfaces (BadgeTileRow) and keep select(*) only on /badges/[slug], the one route that renders the prose. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a component that quietly expected a column now renders an empty value, so make the narrower row its own type so the omission is a compile error rather than a blank tile. Why: The catalog grid is the highest-traffic page on the site and it currently ships every badge's long description text to render a tile. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Cursor pagination for the catalog

Details

listBadges() uses .range() (an OFFSET) plus count exact, so a page deep into /badges makes PostgREST walk and discard every preceding row and count the whole filtered set on each request. Replace ?page= with a keyset cursor ordered on the sort column with id as the tie-break, return nextCursor, and keep the current ?page= handling as a redirect for old links. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a keyset cursor over a non-unique sort column (owner_count, end_date) skips or repeats rows at page boundaries, so the id tie-break is mandatory and the stale-page redirect must stay. Why: Deep pages and crawler walks of the catalog are exactly where an offset starts to cost, and this site has hundreds of badges in every filter combination. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Preconnect the badge CDN and hero

Details

BadgeImage always sets loading=lazy, including the 96 px hero on a badge page, and the document never declares a preconnect to static-cdn.jtvnw.net, so the largest contentful paint pays a fresh DNS and TLS handshake after hydration. Add preconnect and dns-prefetch in the locale layout head, and use fetchpriority=high with eager loading for the badge-detail hero and the first grid row while keeping everything else lazy. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: marking a whole grid eager competes with the CSS for bandwidth and makes the paint worse, so restrict the eager set to the hero and the first row. Why: The badge image is the point of a badge page, and today it starts loading late and behind a cold connection. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

One data cache behind eleven locales

Details

A badge's row, its owner history and its category list are identical in all 11 message locales, yet each locale's route is a separate ISR entry that runs the same queries, so the catalog's database work is paid up to eleven times for one answer. Compute the locale-independent data once (unstable_cache keyed on slug and params only, never on the locale) and let each locale's render translate only the text around it. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a cache key that includes or omits the locale by accident serves one language's copy of a localized field, so classify which columns are genuinely locale-independent before sharing them. Why: Eleven locales are the site's biggest multiplier on catalog cost, and almost none of the data behind them differs by language. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Write a stats row only when it moves

Details

runPotatSync inserts into badge_stats for every badge on every run, so the 15-minute cadence writes roughly 45,000 rows a day that mostly repeat the previous value, and the owner-trend chart has to filter flat lines. Gate the insert on an actual change in owner_count or active_count so the series records events, and keep one slow heartbeat row per badge per day so a flat badge is still visibly alive. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a chart that only plots changes makes a paused poll look like a missing series, so keep the daily heartbeat point and let the chart interpolate between it and the last change. Why: The time series is the site's fastest-growing table, and most of what it stores is the absence of news. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Server-Timing headers on the catalog routes

Details

Nothing on a rendered page says how long its database work took, so the claim that a badge page is slow cannot be attributed without reproducing it locally. Emit a Server-Timing header from the query helper (one db;dur= per read plus a total) on /badges, /badges/[slug], /stats and /leaderboards so the browser's network panel answers the question directly. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a timing header on a publicly cached response can leak internal table names or query shape, so emit whole-millisecond durations with a generic label and never a table or bind value. Why: Performance work on this site needs a measurement that reaches a real visitor's browser, not a local build. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Log the slow reads and show them

Details

The failures are logged and the heartbeats carry per-run totals, but a read that takes 900 ms and succeeds is invisible, and that is the class that turns into a slow site without ever erroring. Have the query helper record label, duration and row count for reads over a threshold into a small table or the next heartbeat payload, and list the slowest of the last hour on the admin status tab. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a per-read write doubles the database traffic exactly when it is already slow, so sample rather than log every read and apply the same retention rule to the table. Why: The expensive-but-successful read is invisible today, and it is the one that degrades the site without a single alert. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Record real-user page timings

Details

The site already has an anonymous beacon (AnalyticsBeacon to /api/track) and a charted /stats page, but no timing signal, so the site feels slow is not measurable per route or per locale. Extend the beacon payload with LCP, INP and CLS from small passive PerformanceObservers, store them alongside the existing analytics_events fields and chart the p75 next to the sync metrics. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an observer running on every paint adds main-thread work to the pages it is measuring, so use passive observers, sample the collection and stop once the page is hidden. Why: The site has traffic analytics and uptime already; field performance is the missing half of the same picture. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

One analytics beacon per page view

Details

AnalyticsBeacon posts to /api/track once on mount (the view) and again on pagehide (the duration), so analytics_events receives two rows per view and every /stats aggregate has to filter them apart. Emit a single row on visibilitychange or pagehide carrying the duration, with a short timer as a fallback for a visit that never fires an unload event. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a pagehide-only beacon loses the visit entirely when the browser kills a tab, undercounting exactly the bounces the dashboard is watching, so keep the timer fallback and compare daily view counts before and after. Why: Halving the largest write volume on the site costs one client change and makes the traffic numbers simpler, not weaker. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

A seq-scan advisor over catalog queries

Details

The indexes in 0001_init and 0009 were chosen by reading the code and nothing shows when the planner stops using one, so a query can regress to a sequential scan and stay there unnoticed. Add a script that runs EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) for the top catalog queries (the /badges filter and sort combinations, the badge_stats history read, the feed, the leaderboards and the momentum view) against the seeded local database and prints every sequential scan with its row estimate and the index it should have used. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an advisor that indexes whatever it sees recommends duplicate indexes on tiny tables and slows every write, so require a minimum estimated row count and make the tool report, never create. Why: A read-heavy catalog on free infrastructure lives or dies by whether its hot queries use an index, and today nobody can tell. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Data-driven nav priority per viewport

Details

The header renders all thirteen destinations only at the xl breakpoint; on a 1024 px laptop nine of them disappear behind a hamburger, and nobody chose which nine. Rank the links by real traffic using the anonymous analytics table, which already stores path, client class and screen width, then take the top six as a primary nav and put the rest in a labelled More menu, recomputing the ranking from a monthly query rather than by hand. Keep the hand-written order in site settings as an override so an admin can pin a link the data would demote. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the nav reshuffles between visits and a noisy month hides a destination the team considers core. Why: It removes real navigation friction on the screen size most visitors use, using traffic data the site already collects instead of a guess. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Multi-select category and rarity filters

Details

Category and rarity are single select elements, so show me Sub and Prime badges is impossible without two passes. Turn both into multi-select chip facets with OR semantics inside a facet and AND across facets, serialised as repeated query parameters (category=sub&category=prime) so a filtered view stays shareable and the back button works. The result count, pagination and every link builder read the same parameter shape. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: repeated parameters need explicit handling in queries.ts and in every link builder or a filter silently vanishes on page two, and OR across two facets can return a set so large the page is pointless. Why: Real collecting questions span categories, and forcing one facet value per query makes the catalog answer the wrong question. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Bedienbarkeit Quick Win

Locale-aware collation for name sorting

Details

The name sort and the category list come from Postgres under the database default collation, so accented and non-Latin categories such as Animaux or Anderungen and the CJK category names order by code point instead of by the reader's alphabet. Sort the category list with an Intl.Collator built from the request locale (the list is small and cacheable), and for the name sort either add a collation to the ORDER BY or sort the loaded page with the same collator while labelling it as page-local. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a locale collation on the sort column can be far slower than the b-tree, and page-local sorting makes page two disagree with page one, so the label must be honest. Why: An alphabetical index that is not alphabetical in the reader's language is a small daily irritation in ten of eleven locales. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Details

When the Supabase refresh token has been rotated away the client loses writes silently and the member only notices when a save fails. Details

Performance Quick Win

Split the sitemap into a chunked index

Details

src/app/sitemap.ts returns one document containing every badge times 11 locales plus every post, game and static page, roughly six thousand entries rebuilt from a paged scan of badges on each revalidation. Move to Next's generateSitemaps so /sitemap.xml becomes an index of per-locale or per-chunk children, which also lets each child carry a real per-badge lastModified. Effort: M. Streaming badge detail with bounded provider waits

The custom-badge protection skips a provider badge that would collide on (set_id, version). The logic is in place and verified at the data layer, but no collision exists today, so the skip has never actually fired. Worth a deliberate test the next time a provider reuses a set id. Effort: low.

Badges Quick Win

Art-Style Atlas

Details

An ML pass over the badge images classifies each into an art-style tag (pixel art, flat vector, gradient/mesh, photo-derived, era-of-release) and a new browse mode lets users explore the catalog by style and see how Twitch's badge art direction changed year over year. Each badge card shows its tags, and filter URLs are shareable. Why it fits: The catalog holds every global badge image, and the images are the only artifact on the site never actually analyzed — this is a derived dataset no competitor has, and it doubles as a visual-history feature. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: A noisy classifier that mistags noticeably different styles destroys trust in every other derived metric on the site, so low- confidence tags must be hidden rather than shown as fact. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Badges Quick Win

Badge DNA Barcode

Details

Every badge gets a deterministic visual barcode (a small striped glyph) encoding a canonical hash of its rarity vector, category and release date. The barcode appears on the badge OG card and can be scanned from a phone camera or pasted into a lookup box to open that badge — and comparing two barcodes visually shows how close two badges are in the rarity space. Why it fits: Rarity is a fixed 4-component formula over data the site already owns, so a stable fingerprint is cheap to define; it gives every badge a physical, shareable identity and turns the OG pipeline into an interactive entry point. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: Encoding a hash of a *mutable* rarity score means the barcode silently changes when a badge's owner count moves — the fingerprint must be over immutable identity fields or it invalidates every shared card. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Badges Quick Win

Badge Half-Life

Details

For every badge, plot the owner curve from the first sighting and measure how long it took to reach half of its lifetime owners — a decay constant. Badge pages show it as "half-life: 4 days" with a small sparkline, and the catalog gets a sortable "fastest burn / slowest burn" list. Why it fits: `badge_stats` already stores a per-badge owner time series every 15 minutes, so half-life is arithmetic on data we already have. It turns a static owner count into a statement about how a badge was actually farmed — a genuinely new axis nobody publishes. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: badges with short histories or noisy potat samples produce nonsense half-lives, so the fit needs a minimum-sample and goodness-of-fit gate before anything is displayed. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-02.md

Badges Quick Win

Rarity Promotion & Demotion Ticker

Details

A live feed of tier crossings — "Badge X just fell from Legendary to Epic as 12,000 players claimed it." Each entry links to the badge with a before/after TBRI breakdown explaining which input moved. Why it fits: `computeRarity` runs on every sync and the changelog infrastructure already exists; a tier change is a diff on a value we recompute every 15 minutes. It makes an invisible background process visible and dramatic. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: threshold jitter can emit the same badge flapping between two tiers several times a day; it needs hysteresis or a per-badge cooldown before writing a ticker row. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-02.md

Orphan Badge Watch

Details

A small rotating exhibit of badges at the bottom of the owner distribution — zero, one or two recorded owners — each with its story and a "submit what you know" nudge. Basically an adoption page for the catalog's loneliest entries. Why it fits: potat owner counts and the TBRI owner-scarcity input already identify the tail; the page is a filtered list plus editorial copy. It surfaces the most interesting rows in the database, which today are buried at the end of a sort. Effort: S. Badges Quick Win

Economy ledger (XP and BadgesCoins transaction history)

Details

A profile sub-page listing every XP and coin movement with a timestamp, source (daily login, badge unlocked, wheel, game, heist, coin rain), delta and resulting balance, filterable by source and date range, with a running-balance small chart. Also a per-source summary ("earned this month from games: N"). Why it fits: XP and coins are awarded by at least five subsystems and every award already writes an event (the `/feed` shows them for all users). Badges Quick Win

Data freshness badges and stale-catalog banner

Details

Every catalog surface (grid, badge detail, leaderboard, stats) shows an unobtrusive "catalog updated X ago · owners X ago" line sourced from the newest heartbeat/sync timestamp, and when the newest successful sync is older than the expected cadence the header shows a dismissible stale-data banner with a link to `/status` or the changelog. Why it fits: The project already records `system_heartbeats` and prunes them after 90 days, and the 15-minute potat cadence means data age is a real, user-visible quantity when a cron fails. Silent staleness is exactly the failure the heartbeat table was built to expose. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: Thresholds hard-coded to the dev sync interval make the banner fire permanently in production (or never), training users to ignore it. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-03.md

Recently viewed and remembered filters

Details

Clearing is one click. Why it fits: The catalog is paginated and filtered by rarity, category and status; re-deriving the same view on every visit is pure friction. The viewed set can also seed "back to the badge whose window is closing". Effort: S. Badges Quick Win

New-collector onboarding checklist

Details

A dismissible checklist card on the dashboard for accounts without progress: link Twitch (if not yet), sync your badges, claim the daily login, spin the wheel once, set a profile display name, add your first wishlist item. Each step deep-links to the surface that completes it and is checked off from real state, not from a click. Content Quick Win

Community Field Notes

Details

A crowdsourced wiki layer over badges whose drop window, category or how-to-earn is missing or vague in the badgebase feed: collectors submit field notes ("drops in the Twitch mobile app only"), others confirm or dispute. Confirmed notes are shown on the badge page with a contributor name and a reputation counter. Why it fits: the catalog already has gaps by design (badgebase is the authoritative source but not exhaustive); an in-house reputation system extends the existing XP model to data work, not just games. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: bad-faith or malicious edits to earn XP — needs moderation queues, sources required, and no XP for unconfirmed notes. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-04.md

Details

A collector can send BadgesCoins to another with a public thank-you message, and every accepted gift appears on a site-wide "gifts" wall with both names and the amount. A weekly top-gifter strip gives the wall its own small leaderboard. Badges Quick Win

Claim-path start lag

Details

For each window, measure the gap between the listed opening time and the first owner increase observed in badge_stats, and show it on the badge page as 'first claims tracked 47 minutes after the listed start', with a per-category median and a filter for windows that never delivered a tracked claim. This exposes claim-path delay and quietly broken drops without guessing at Twitch internals. Uses window start dates plus the owner series and sync timestamps. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a sync that simply ran late makes a window look delayed, so each gap must record how long after the window the observation was actually taken. Why: A window that opens late or never opens is the difference between a collector claiming a badge and missing it. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Provider page snapshot archive

Details

At first sighting of a badge, and again when its window closes, store the provider page we read it from (HTML plus a rendered screenshot) against the badge, and let the detail page link to that frozen copy with its fetch timestamp. When badgebase or potat later changes or deletes a page, the evidence this site based a window date on still exists and can be diffed against the current one. Storage is small text and one image per badge per event. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: archived third-party pages can contain personal data or tracking scripts, so the snapshot must be sanitized before serving and never rendered with scripts enabled. Why: A badge database is only as credible as the evidence behind its dates, and provider pages disappear. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Community tags on badges

Any logged-in member can add short free-form tags to a badge ('nostalgia', 'ugly', 'subscribe-and-forget') from a suggestion list, and each tag gets a page listing every badge carrying it, ordered by rarity, plus a tag cloud on the catalog. Moderation reuses the existing admin content tools; no other list on the site is a shared vocabulary over the catalog. Effort: M. Badges Quick Win

Badge art asset audit

Details

A pass records which resolutions exist for each badge (1x, 2x, 4x), the byte size, and whether the canonical URL still returns an image, then publishes a 'blurry' and a 'broken art' filter with an admin list of badges whose art is missing or dead. Dead URLs get a single re-resolve attempt against the catalog before they are recorded as broken, and missing higher resolutions get a distinct placeholder treatment instead of an upscaled blur. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a transient CDN failure marks hundreds of badges broken, so failures need a retry and a cool-down before they are stored. Why: A catalog judged on its artwork should know which of its images are missing, dead or low-resolution. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Claim plan for open windows

Details

A member gets a persistent claim plan: the open windows they care about, ordered by a server-computed combination of deadline and rarity, each with a state of want, claimed or skipped that survives across visits and devices, plus a progress ring and an exportable checklist. It is stateful per member, unlike the stateless claimable filter, and it is silent, unlike wishlist alerts. Uses open windows, member wishlist data and synced ownership to auto-tick claims that landed. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: if a claimed tick depends on the next inventory sync rather than the member, the plan shows false progress, so the manual state and the synced state must be stored and displayed separately. Why: Collectors juggling several live windows need a plan they can trust across days, not a fresh filter every visit. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Provisional rarity labelling

Details

Badges younger than a configurable observation window, or backed by fewer than N owner samples, show their TBRI as 'provisional' with a distinct chip, are excluded from tier-crossing alerts and tier-based leaderboards until they mature, and the detail page states when the estimate should settle. Uses the age of the badge's badge_stats series. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: hiding provisional badges instead of labelling them removes new drops from the catalog at exactly the moment people are searching for them. Why: Rarity computed from three days of data is a guess, and presenting it as a tier misleads everyone who reads it. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Rarity tier cutoff calibration audit

Details

Recompute the live distribution of TBRI values and check that the tier cutoffs still produce the pyramid the tiers claim: how many Legendaries actually exist, whether a band is empty or overflowing, and what share of the catalog sits in each tier. Publishes the cutoff table, the current shares and a proposed recut when the distribution has drifted, stored per run so the drift is visible over time. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: automatic recuts would invalidate every screenshot and ranking overnight, so the audit may only propose; cutoffs change deliberately and are versioned. Why: Rarity tiers are the catalog's shared vocabulary and nothing currently checks that the labels still match the thresholds defining them. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Evergreen vs limited lifecycle

Details

Derive a lifecycle state per badge, never yet closed (evergreen, like subscriber badges), closed at least once (limited), or returned (reissued), show it as a chip on the tile and detail page, and add it to the catalog facets so permanent and limited are browsable classes. Distinct from the claimable-now filter, which only sees today's state. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a limited badge that simply has not closed yet is mislabelled evergreen, so the chip must say 'no recorded end' whenever that is all the data supports. Why: Whether a badge is permanent or a one-time drop is the first question a collector asks about it. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Quick Win

Catalog records board

Details

A records page computed over the whole catalog: fastest to 100k owners, longest continuously open window, largest set by version count, rarest badge still claimable, oldest badge still obtainable, biggest single-sync owner jump, most owners lost to a provider revision. Each record links to the badge and states the observation window it was computed over, with the date it was set. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: records over a short history are artefacts of when this site started syncing, so every record must disclose its window or it will silently mean 'since we launched'. Why: The catalog contains superlatives nobody has ever computed and records are readable at a glance. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Claim funnel per badge

Instrument the badge page as a small funnel: page views, expand of the claim instructions, clicks on the claim link, and confirmed owned-afterwards from the next inventory sync, all from anonymous analytics plus link-click logging, with an admin view of the badges with the most interest and the fewest conversions. It answers which badges people want and cannot get. Effort: M. Content Quick Win

Per-badge flavor blurbs

Details

Every badge gets a short human-written line — one or two sentences of context or character, deliberately separate from the dry how-to-earn instructions — rendered on the tile tooltip, the detail page and the OG card. Blurbs are a column on the badge row authored in the content tab, and any badge without one falls back to a generated line assembled from its category, tier and release year so coverage is never zero. Collectors can rate a blurb's accuracy with a single tap, feeding a queue for admins to revise the weakest ones. Data: authored text plus catalog fields for the fallback. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: 476 hand-written lines become a maintenance debt and a wrong blurb about how a badge was earned reads as authoritative; keep blurb and how-to-earn strictly separate and label generated fallbacks as generated. Why: Unique prose on every badge page is both the difference between an index and a guide, and the only original text search engines can rank. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Quick Win

Zero-result search to gap briefs

Details

Every catalog search returning nothing is logged with its query, locale and timestamp, and a weekly job clusters the queries into themes (prime gaming, twitchcon, mod badge) and turns each cluster into a short brief: what people searched for, whether a near-match exists in the catalog, and which page should answer it. Themes with no answering page become tutorial or glossary candidates; themes where a badge exists but is unfindable become alias or synonym work on the search index. Data: the logged queries plus the catalog's name, set_id and category fields for near-match detection. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: the log becomes an unmoderated dump of whatever a visitor typed into a box; store a trimmed query string only, and drop anything resembling a name or email address. Why: A failed search is the most honest statement of what content the site is missing, and the site already collects it by doing nothing. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Quick Win

Alt text for badge art

Details

A generated accessible name and alt text for every badge image across the site: the badge's name, its set and category and its rarity tier, assembled from the row instead of left empty or as a filename. Where a badge has a curated flavor blurb, that enriches the description, and the Open Graph route uses the same string in its metadata. Data: badge name, set, category, tier and the optional blurb. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: hundreds of near-identical strings simply restate the visible name and make the screen-reader experience worse; keep only the informative fields, skip the decorative tier chip duplication, and mark purely decorative instances empty. Why: Badge art is the entire content of the catalog, so its accessibility is not a nicety but the difference between a usable and an unusable page for a screen-reader visitor. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json
