Content Quick Win

Human content index page

Details

An HTML index listing every piece of writing the site has — posts grouped by month, tutorials, glossary terms, methodology and sources pages, curated lists — as a browsable, crawlable directory, deliberately separate from the XML sitemap's machine purpose. It gives a reader the whole archive at a glance, gives crawlers and internal-link auditing a single hub, and can show each content type's coverage count so a gap is visible. Data: content rows grouped by type and date plus the route list. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: it grows into a thousand-link page that is useless as navigation and slow to render; paginate by type and keep it a hub rather than a dump. Why: An archive nobody can browse is an archive nobody links to, and internal links are what carry the site's ranking between its own pages. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Quick Win

Inline editorial review threads

Details

Drafts in the content tab support inline anchored comments: an editor selects a sentence and leaves a note, the author replies in the same thread, and the thread must be resolved before the publish button unlocks. It replaces out-of-band review and keeps the discussion attached to the exact text it concerns, with each thread typed as fact, structure, SEO or legal so a writer knows what kind of change is being asked for. Data: comment rows anchored to a character range of a content revision, plus the existing content status fields. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it becomes a second chat and the publish gate is resented; keep threads short-lived, auto-resolve them on publish, and never block a typo fix on an open structural thread. Why: Editorial quality on a site written by more than one person depends on review happening in the document rather than in a chat window. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Design Quick Win

Motion token scale with a motion-level setting

Details

Each animation picks its own duration and easing, and the only control over motion is the prefers-reduced-motion media query. Define a motion token scale (--dur-1/2/3, --ease-standard/enter/exit) consumed by the component classes, plus an explicit level in the interface, full, subtle or off, persisted like the theme and mapped to a data-motion attribute on the html element. This is a different axis from the reduced-motion idea already on the board, which honours the OS query: this gives a visitor who wants calm but not zero motion a middle setting, and makes the decorative set (sparkles, coin rain, particles) droppable while keeping transitions that carry meaning. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: tokens every component ignores in favour of hardcoded numbers, or an off level that also kills the transition that communicates a state change. Why: Motion is part of this site's identity, all sparkles and flying coins, so it deserves the same token discipline as colour and radius. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

One overlay primitive for dialogs and sheets

Details

Dialogs, game result panels, the language panel, the mobile nav sheet and the account menu are separate implementations with different scrims, sizes and dismissal rules. Define one overlay primitive with three presentations chosen by viewport and content size, anchored popover, centred dialog and bottom sheet on phones, sharing a scrim token, scroll lock, escape and outside-click dismissal, a title-and-close header pattern and safe-area insets for the sheet. This is the layout and behaviour contract; the focus-trap work already on the board slots into it instead of being rebuilt per surface. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: a sheet that traps page scroll on iOS, an overlay rendered inside a stacking context under a card, or a half-finished migration leaving two scrim colours on the same page. Why: Overlays are where the games, the account and the language all live, and five different scrims is five different products. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Optical normalization of badge art

Details

Badge PNGs have wildly different internal padding and content bounds, a Bits badge fills its canvas while a Partner badge floats in it, so a grid of uniformly sized images still reads as a grid of differently sized badges. Add a sync-time measurement of each image's content bounding box, store the inset alongside the badge row, and render tiles scaling the art so the content occupies a consistent optical box rather than the file filling a fixed one. The source art is untouched and the measurement is one pass per image at sync. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: art upscaled past its native resolution until it blurs, or a trim that clips a glow or shadow that is genuinely part of the badge. Why: The whole page is a wall of one object, so the eye reads any size inconsistency as sloppiness before it reads anything else. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Light-theme contrast plate for badge art

Details

Many badge assets are drawn with white or very light strokes on transparency, which reads beautifully on the dark surface and nearly vanishes on the light one. Store a cheap per-image luminance-and-extent measure at sync, and in the light theme render a subtle plate, a surface-2 fill or a low-opacity hairline outline, behind only the art that needs it, so light-stroke badges stay legible while everything else keeps its current unplated look. The decision is per image, not per theme. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a plate on art that did not need one reading as a rendering bug, or a plate strong enough that the light theme becomes a grid of grey squares. Why: Half the catalog is light-on-transparent and the light theme currently loses exactly that half of the wall. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Explicit data-sufficiency states for charts

Details

Charts render the same way with one point, four points or a year of history, and a gap in the sync cadence draws a straight line through it as if nothing were missing. Define the sufficiency states explicitly: one point shows a large value with no-history-yet rather than a flat line, fewer than a week of points shows dots with no trend line and no momentum claim, a gap longer than the sync cadence renders as a dashed segment with a no-data marker, and an empty series shows a designed placeholder instead of bare axes. Thresholds live with the chart contract, distinct from page-level empty states because this is the chart telling the truth about its own inputs. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: hiding a real trend behind a conservative threshold, or a dashed gap overstating how much is missing when it was one skipped heartbeat. Why: Every one of these charts is built from a polling sync, and pretending such a series is complete is the easiest way to mislead a collector. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Data-saver mode for slow and metered connections

Details

A visitor on a metered connection gets the full experience and pays for it: decorative motion, particle effects, the chart bundle and 4x art. Add a designed data-saver mode, auto-enabled from the Save-Data header and the connection effective type and overridable in settings, that drops decorative motion and ambient effects to the subtle level, requests 1x art, defers the stats bundle until a chart approaches the viewport, and replaces coin-rain style effects with static equivalents, surfaced as a small chip so the visitor understands why the site looks different rather than assuming it is broken. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the mode stripping something functional along with the decoration, or auto-detection misreading a connection and making the site feel diminished for everyone. Why: This is a data-heavy catalog visited daily by people on phones, and a way to pay less for it is a feature rather than a degradation. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Spiele Quick Win

Catalog Match Three

Details

Badge tiles fall in a six-column grid and a swap is legal only when both tiles share a category, so every move is a small act of catalog sorting. Clears score by the tier of the matched badges, and the server replays the submitted move log against the round seed to recompute the score before paying. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: score is the most forgeable number on the site, so the move log must be the only thing trusted and any client-displayed score must be clearly advisory. Why: A match-three loop and the badge catalog reinforce each other instead of one being a skin over the other. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Add-ons Quick Win

Round share card

Details

A dedicated OG route renders one round as a share card: the game, its badge-themed visual, bet, payout, net, and a footer linking to the verifiable round record. It appears as a 'share this round' action on the result panel and in the round tape, and the image is cached per round id like the other OG routes. The existing cards cover badges and profiles, not the one artifact a player actually wants to brag about. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: satori re-renders per crawler fetch, so the route must be immutable-cached on the round id and must not expose another member's round id to a logged-out visitor unless that round was explicitly published. Why: Big wins are the arcade's most shareable output and currently leave the site as a screenshot or nothing. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Quick Win

Personal RTP and variance report

Details

For each game a member has played, the report computes actual return (payout sum over bet sum), the expected return implied by the published payout distribution, the difference in standard deviations, and a hot or cold flag that always states the sample size. It is a computed layer over game_rounds and the engine constants, not another counter, and it plainly says when the sample is too small to mean anything. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a report that calls a normal swing an unlucky streak trains superstition, so the z-score must ship with its confidence band and the wording must never imply the games owe a member a correction. Why: The site publishes the odds, and the natural counterpart is showing each member what those odds actually did to their own coins. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Quick Win

Scheduled arcade nights

Details

The panel schedules themed hours ('Slots Hour', 'Heist Hour') on a published calendar; during a night the featured game gets a raised maximum bet and its own hourly board, and finishing on that board pays a catalog-linked cosmetic. The schedule is a settings row, the boards are computed from game_rounds in the window, and the page shows the next three nights in the member's local time. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: hourly boards invite all-night botting, so the one-round-per-second limit stays in force and the board should reward placement rather than raw round count, or the winner is simply whoever stayed awake. Why: The arcade is always-on and never scheduled, so nothing gives members a reason to show up at the same time as each other. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Quick Win

Per-game keyboard hotkey maps

Details

Each game gets a documented key map - number keys for a quiz answer, arrows for the vault, space to cash out, enter to bet the remembered amount - shown in a collapsible legend inside the panel and remembered per member. The keys drive the same controls the mouse does, so no new game logic is needed, and the legend ships through the localized messages. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: keys must not fire while a text field or modal has focus, or typing in an input plays rounds, so the handler belongs behind the existing modal focus trap. Why: Several of the 13 games are keyboard-shaped (quiz, memory, vault) and currently waste that by requiring a mouse. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Statistiken Quick Win

Duplicate-Artwork Detector

Details

Cluster catalog rows by a perceptual hash of the badge PNG plus a normalized set-and-name key, and surface probable duplicates or re-releases with a similarity score and side-by-side thumbnails in an admin queue. Reads the badges table (set_id, version, image_url). Effort: L. Risk if built badly: legitimate variant badges across versions share artwork, so clusters must require admin confirmation and never auto-merge. Why: Keeps the catalog trustworthy, which is the entire product for a badge database. Source: new-ideas-06-stats.json

Profil Quick Win

Collection Cover and Dedication

Details

A collection gets a cover: a title ('The Long Winter'), an optional one-line dedication, and one owned badge chosen as the cover mark, rendered as a header band above the shelves and into the profile OG image. All three fields sit in customization with hard size limits, and the cover badge must resolve against the member's latest synced inventory so a stale claim cannot point at a badge they do not own. The catalog pages keep the facts; this is the member's own framing. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: an unmoderated title and dedication become a defacement surface, and a skipped ownership check lets a member headline a badge they never earned. Why: Naming a collection is the cheapest way to make a profile feel authored rather than generated. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Thirty-Day Commitment Pin

Details

A member pins one badge above the showcase for a fixed 30-day term, and the pin cannot be swapped or removed until the term expires; the profile shows 'pinned 12/30 days' next to it. Implementation is one row per profile with a locked_until timestamp, a swap endpoint that rejects early changes, and a logged admin unlock for genuine mistakes. The value is the cost: the pin says this badge, not this week's favourite. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: the lock becomes a trap when a member wants the badge gone, so an audited admin release path must exist from day one. Why: A signal nobody can cheaply change is worth more than another free text field on a profile. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Verified Collector Oaths

Details

A member swears one standing collecting rule from a fixed vocabulary — 'I claim no paid badges', 'I never break a set', 'I always claim TwitchCon badges' — plus an optional free clause, and the site re-verifies it against the synced inventory on every run, showing the oath as kept or broken with the date it broke. A broken oath stays visible until a new one is sworn. Verification needs only badges the member already has, so it is one pass inside the existing potat sync. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a verification edge case brands honest members as oath-breakers, so every verdict must come with the list of rows that produced it. Why: A self-declared collecting principle that the server actually checks is a trust signal no profile field can fake. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Rejection Pile Shelf

Details

A deliberately unflattering second shelf: badges the member owns but has chosen not to display, each tagged with a one-word reason from a fixed list ('common', 'grindy', 'ugly', 'region-locked'). It renders collapsed under the showcase and makes a collection an edited opinion rather than a dump. Because reasons are chips rather than free text, nothing new needs moderating. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: the pile reads as a public insult to designers, so the vocabulary stays neutral and no badge-level aggregate of rejections is ever published. Why: What a collector refuses to show says more about taste than what they show, and no badge site currently captures it. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Opt-in Owner Roster

Details

On a badge page, members whose inventory is public can appear in a sparse roster — avatar, level, 'owned since' — beside a 'recently claimed' strip, ordered by claim recency rather than owner count. The roster is opted into once per member through a single toggle, is paginated, and reads the visibility flag at render time. It is the catalog pointed back at people, the mirror of a public collection page. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it exposes ownership of members who meant to stay private, so the surface must exist only for explicit opt-ins and re-check the flag on every render. Why: It closes the loop between a badge and the collectors who have it, which is the discovery the catalog alone cannot give. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Guest Curator Week

Details

A member can hand a friend the featured shelf for a week: the guest chooses which of the owner's badges are shown, writes a short curatorial line, and is bylined on the profile for the duration. Picks must be badges the owner actually has, the owner can veto individual ones, and the arrangement expires automatically leaving a dated note behind. It is a two-person creative act, not a schedule change. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the curatorial line is free text under someone else's name, so it needs a length cap, the veto and only one active curator at a time. Why: Letting a friend curate your shelf is a social ritual that produces content neither member could make alone. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Follow a Collector

Details

Members can follow each other to receive a narrow, chosen set of signals — a tier-crossing unlock, a level bracket, a completed set, a public milestone — never every claim. Followers and following are counted separately on the profile, the relationship is private by default with a public toggle, and a follower gets at most one digest notification per member per day. The activity feed stays site-wide; this is the member-scoped slice of it. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it becomes a notification engagement loop and a popularity score; the digest cap and the event scoping are what keep it useful instead of noisy. Why: Following is the missing primitive of a community site — you cannot currently say 'I want to know what this collector does'. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Badge Pen Pals

Details

An opt-in pairing where the site matches two members whose collections are complementary — each owns a large share of what the other lacks — without revealing who they are, and the pair exchanges curated gap lists of five badges through a structured form. After two exchanges either side can reveal, and if both agree the profiles are linked as pen pals; otherwise the pairing simply ends. The matching uses the same overlap arithmetic the compare view already needs. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: in a small member base matches identify people by elimination, so pairing requires a minimum candidate pool and must never show a partial match. Why: Complementary collections are the most interesting relationship the data can find, and anonymity makes it approachable. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Nominated Custodian Continuity

Details

A member can nominate a custodian who, after 180 days without a sync or login, may take over the public side of the dormant profile: keeping the featured shelf current, maintaining the cover and answering the guestbook, clearly labelled as a custodian. Ownership, inventory and private data are never transferred, the custodian sees exactly what a visitor sees, and the original member reclaiming the account ends the arrangement immediately. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a custodian misrepresents someone or quietly edits history, so the label must be prominent, every custodian action public, and reclaim instant. Why: Active collections going silent is a real loss when the showcases are the site's content, and consent-based continuity preserves them without taking anything away. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Per-Badge Visibility Controls

Details

Beyond the single public-or-private inventory switch, a member can mark individual badges as spotlight — visible and verified-owned even while the rest of the inventory stays private — or hidden, excluded from a public collection. The controls are per badge, capped (say 20 spotlights), and resolved by one shared function every inventory-rendering surface calls, so the same badge behaves consistently on profiles, the owner roster and guest slots. It lets a private collector take part in social features without opening everything. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a surface forgets the resolver and a hidden badge leaks through a cached page, so the shared resolver must be mandatory rather than best-effort. Why: The current private-or-public toggle forces an all-or-nothing choice that keeps private collectors out of the social layer entirely. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Pseudonymous Participation Profile

Details

A member can create one pseudonym used on the social surfaces — guestbook stamps, relay hops, race entries, review windows, claim pools — while their Twitch handle stays attached to their own pages; the two are linked internally and by no public route. The pseudonym carries its own display name and level chip, can be retired at will, and interactions it made keep the pseudonym rather than retroactively revealing identity. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: pseudonyms become a shield for bad behaviour, so the internal link must exist for moderation, only one pseudonym per account is allowed, and abuse revokes it publicly. Why: It lets shy or modest members join the social layer without attaching everything they do to their Twitch identity. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Audience Preview Mode

Details

From the customizer a member can preview their own profile as four audiences — a stranger, a signed-in member, a follower, a muted visitor — and each preview renders exactly the fields that audience would see, with hidden ones replaced by a boundary marker rather than omitted silently. The same audience resolver drives the real render, so preview and reality cannot drift apart. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a second code path makes the preview reassuring while the live page leaks, so the resolver must be shared, not reimplemented. Why: Privacy controls are only trustworthy if members can see what they actually produce, and this is the cheapest way to make them trustworthy. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Identity Continuity and Name History

Details

The profile keeps a name history: when a sync sees a different login for the same account, the previous handle is stored and shown as 'formerly known as', old profile URLs keep resolving through an alias table, and the numeric ordinal permalink stays permanent. The member can hide the history from visitors while the links still resolve, and a rename is only accepted when it comes from the same authenticated account. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: alias resolution merges two accounts into one identity or publicly exposes a rename the member wanted forgotten, so matching must be authenticated and hiding must be a member choice. Why: Twitch handles change between drops, and without an identity record every old link and every old reputation dies with the name. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Quick Win

Inventory Health Check

Details

A member-facing diagnostic over their own synced inventory: badges no longer present in the catalog, duplicate image UUIDs across two set ids, badges whose set was dissolved, rows whose last sync is much older than the rest, and any gap between inventory counts and the public collection. Every finding links to the badge or the relevant how-to-earn line, and the panel separates 'we think this is a data problem' from 'we think this is your account'. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it alarms members about normal catalog churn, so findings must be phrased as possibilities with the underlying rows shown. Why: Members are the first to notice a sync looking wrong, and giving them a diagnostic beats every bug report that currently starts with a screenshot. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Performance Quick Win

Estimated counts for text-search pagination

Details

When ?q= is set, listBadges runs three ilike conditions plus an exact COUNT over badges before returning a single page, so the heaviest query on the site answers only how many pages exist. Request an estimated count from PostgREST when q is present and let Pagination render about N badges instead of an exact total. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: an estimate that disagrees with the pager's arithmetic produces a dead last page, so clamp the requested page to the window that actually returned rows rather than to the estimate. Why: Search is the main way people find a specific badge, and its pagination count currently costs more than the search itself. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

One read per badge page for metadata

Details

generateMetadata() and the page body on /badges/[slug] (and /blog/[slug]) each call the same lookup, getBadgeBySlug / getPostBySlug, so every ISR render and every cache miss issues two identical PostgREST round trips for one row. Wrap those lookups in React's cache() so one request issues one read. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: cache() must not memoise a failed lookup, or a transient pooler error becomes a 404 for that whole request; cache the resolved row only. Why: Every badge detail page doubles its first database read for no benefit, which is pure cost on the site's second-busiest route. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Downsample the owner-history chart query

Details

getBadgeStatsHistory() fetches up to 250 badge_stats rows for every badge page and maps each into a chart point, but OwnersChart renders roughly 60 readable positions at its width. Ask for a bucketed series (one average per time bucket) or step the series server-side, keeping raw points for the recent window, so both the read and the RSC payload shrink about four-fold. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a coarse bucket hides the claim spike the chart exists to show, so keep the last 24 hours at full resolution and bucket only the older range. Why: The owner-trend chart is on every badge page, and it currently transfers four times more time-series data than any screen can draw. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Run the functions in the database region

Details

next.config.ts sets no function region, so deployments default to one Vercel region while the Supabase pooler configured for this project is aws-1-eu-west-1, meaning every PostgREST read may cross an ocean. /api/health already reports dbLatencyMs and VERCEL_REGION, so pin the functions beside the pooler (or move the pooler) and compare both numbers before and after. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: moving functions away from the audience improves database latency and worsens time-to-first-byte for visitors, so measure both and keep the CDN in front of the cached routes. Why: One configuration line decides whether every query on this read-heavy site makes a transatlantic round trip. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Cache the settings reads in the layout

Details

src/app/[locale]/layout.tsx awaits getFeatures() and getMaintenance() on every render of every route in all 11 locales, and each is a site_settings read that changes only when an admin saves the panel. Wrap both in unstable_cache with a tag that the settings PATCH route invalidates, and keep the maintenance decision on the uncached path. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a cached maintenance flag would leave the site locked after an admin switches it off, so the write must invalidate and the flag should be re-read at the point it is enforced. Why: Two database reads are paid by every visitor on every page for values that change a few times a month. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

A typeahead instead of every badge key

Details

/compare calls getCatalogKeys(), which pages through all of badges in 1000-row batches to build a picker that shows a handful of matches, so the compare page transfers the whole catalog before it can render anything. Replace the picker with a debounced ilike prefix query limited to 20 rows (reusing the existing sanitizeQuery escaping) and keep the paged sweep only where a full list is genuinely exported. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an ilike on user input without the existing wildcard escaping matches everything and turns the typeahead into a full scan, so reuse sanitizeQuery and cap the limit server-side. Why: The two-member compare view is a small feature that currently pays the cost of the entire catalog on every visit. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Pre-render the hottest badge pages

Details

/badges/[slug] declares revalidate=120 but no generateStaticParams, so all roughly 476 badges times 11 locales are built on first hit and the popular ones pay that regeneration repeatedly. Generate the badges with the most owners plus every currently active badge at build time across the locale set, and let the long tail stay on demand. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: pre-rendering thousands of pages at build is what makes next build die with exit code 134 on a small machine, so cap the generated set and keep it out of the growth path. Why: A small hot subset of badge pages absorbs most of the traffic and is the cheapest thing on the site to serve from a prebuilt cache. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

A shorter provider timeout inside a request

Details

perfil.ts, potat.ts, badgebase.ts, ivr.ts and helix.ts all share a single 15-second FETCH_TIMEOUT_MS, so a provider call made while a visitor waits can hold that request for fifteen seconds, far past the point where the failure looks like a broken site. Split the constant into a sync budget (15 s) and a request budget (about 1500 ms), and have the request path fall back to the stored value on timeout. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: too short a request budget makes a slow provider look permanently down and the fallback silently serves old data, so log every timeout with the provider name so the fallback rate is visible. Why: A one-size timeout means the sync and a visitor share the same patience, and only the sync can afford to wait. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Immutable caching for the static assets

Details

/flags/*, icon-192.png, icon-512.png and sw.js are served from public/ with no explicit cache policy, and sw.js is re-fetched on every navigation, which is also how a push-subscription update can fail to land. Set a long immutable policy for the genuinely static assets in next.config.ts and give the service worker a version query or a short revalidation so updates reach devices. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: caching sw.js immutably strands devices on an old worker with no way to receive the fix, so the worker's own policy must stay short while the flags and icons go long. Why: Every visitor downloads flag SVGs and PWA icons that never change, and the service worker in particular must not be cached like them. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Warm the changed pages after a sync

Details

After runGlobalSync or runPotatSync commits, the changed slugs are already known (they are what the engines just wrote), yet the first visitor to each freshly invalidated page pays the regeneration, and that visitor arrives at the exact moment a drop is announced. Have the sync issue a small number of internal fetches for the changed badge URLs so the cache is warm before the traffic peak. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a loop that fetches every changed page from inside the 60-second serverless sync blows the invocation budget, so cap the warm list to new and newly expired badges and run it after the writes commit. Why: New badges are the site's traffic spike, and the current cache makes the first wave of visitors do the regeneration work. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

A resume cursor for a killed sync

Details

The syncs write in 200-row chunks and can be cut off by the 60-second serverless limit mid-run, leaving a half-applied diff that the next tick recomputes from scratch, meaning two sets of provider calls for one outcome. Record a sync_runs row holding the phase and the chunk reached, and have the next run resume from it. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: resuming onto a stale plan applies yesterday's diff to today's catalog, so the cursor must be invalidated when the provider snapshot it was planned from has changed. Why: A timed-out sync currently pays full price twice, and the per-badge sweep is the workload most likely to hit the wall. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Month-partition the event tables

Details

analytics_events and activity_events are the tables that will decide this database's size, and every read of them is a recent-window read while the retention delete has to scan the whole table. Give them native monthly partitions, or at minimum a BRIN index on ts and created_at, so the feed, the stats views and the prune stay cheap as they pass millions of rows. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: partitioning a live table requires rewriting the data and re-creating every policy and grant, so rehearse it against the local seed database and keep the unpartitioned table recoverable until the swap is verified. Why: Partitioning is the one database change that gets harder every day this catalog is live, and the event tables are where it will be needed. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Memoise the achievement stat bundle

Details

Performance Quick Win

A capacity board on the status tab

Details

Performance Quick Win

Batch the fan-out push send

Details

runGlobalSync calls sendPushToAll after a new drop, and that loop over push_subscriptions runs inside the sync's 60-second serverless budget and grows with the member count. Send in chunks with bounded concurrency, let one bad endpoint not abort the run, or move the whole fan-out to a separate invocation the sync triggers. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a batched send that duplicates a notification or drops an endpoint is worse than a slow send, so keep the send idempotent per subscription and badge and prune endpoints the push service rejects. Why: The new-drop notification is the site's best retention feature and it currently shares a budget with the catalog write. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

ETag the changelog RSS feed

Details

Performance Quick Win

Poll the feed with the cursor it returns

Details

FeedList fetches /api/feed?limit=30 every five seconds and re-sends the same thirty events, de-duplicating by id on the client, even though the endpoint already returns nextCursor and accepts a cursor parameter. Send the newest id the client holds and prepend only what came back, so a quiet period transfers an empty array instead of thirty activity rows. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a cursor that is dropped after a hidden tab or a long sleep shows a gap the reader cannot detect, so fall back to a full fetch when the tab was hidden and keep the existing id de-duplication. Why: The live feed is on every page's attention path and currently transfers its whole result set every five seconds forever. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Batch the sync per-badge side effects

Details

runGlobalSync writes badge_events one row at a time and, for every new badge, creates a blog post, a set of notifications and a push inside the same loop, so a large drop becomes a serial chain of round trips inside a 60-second invocation. Collect the events into one insert, publish a single digest post for a multi-badge drop instead of one per badge, and fan the notifications out in one pass. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: replacing a post per badge with a digest changes what the blog feed and the push history show, so keep the per-badge badge_events rows intact so each badge's own provenance page is unaffected. Why: A new drop is the one sync run that also writes content, pushes and notifications, and it is the run most likely to be cut off. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Quick Win

Stop the health probe counting the catalog

Details

Read the total from the same aggregate the catalog views maintain, or from a value the sync refreshes, and keep one cheap bounded read as the reachability signal. Effort: S. Performance Quick Win

Details

Split the messages per namespace and request only the ones a route declares, or at least group the largest namespaces so the parse is proportional to the page. Effort: M. Content Quick Win

Catalog Eras editorial hubs

Details

Named historical periods of badge releases — the closed-beta years, the Prime Gaming era, the Hype Train era, the affiliate-era explosion — each with an editorial page explaining what changed about how badges were earned and a grid of the badges released inside it. Eras are explicit date ranges in a small table (name, slug, start, end, intro copy) and the grid is an ordinary catalog query filtered to that range, so the page stays correct as new badges arrive. Each era links to its predecessor and successor to form a readable history. Data: first-sighting and release dates from badge_events and the catalog, plus authored copy. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the era boundaries are arguable and a wrong one becomes a factual claim the site publishes with authority; keep the ranges in data with a note explaining how they were chosen. Why: It turns a flat catalog of 476 items into a story a newcomer can read in order, which is exactly the narrative the badge world has never had written down. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Learn hub for claiming basics

Details

A /learn section of long-form tutorials answering what a new viewer actually types: what a global badge is, how to claim a Prime badge, why a badge does not appear in the picker, how channel sub badges differ from global ones, and how to check which global badges an account owns. Content Quick Win

Internal-link suggestion engine

Details

While an editor writes a draft, a side panel scans the body for badge names, set names, categories, tier vocabulary and glossary terms and suggests the internal target for each match — a badge detail page, a filtered catalog view, a glossary anchor — with the anchor text taken from the draft itself. It also flags mentions with no target yet (an un-glossed term, a badge absent from the catalog) as content gaps, and reports the draft's internal link count against its length. Data: a term index built from badge names, set ids, categories, FAQ keys and glossary terms, matched against the draft's plain text. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it over-links common words and turns prose into a link farm; require a confidence threshold and let the editor accept suggestions individually rather than auto-inserting them. Why: Internal links are what let 476 badge pages and a growing blog hold each other up in search, and doing it by hand is what nobody does. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Quick Win

Keyword-to-page map

Details

An internal map assigning each target query cluster to exactly one canonical page — a badge detail page, a category landing page, a tutorial, a glossary term — with its current owner page and a cannibalization flag when two pages compete for the same intent. It is generated by matching queries against page titles, headings and copy already in the repo and shows clusters with no page at all as the editorial backlog. Data: per-route text extracted from the content rows plus, where available, the zero-result search log and referrer hosts. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it hardens into a stale spreadsheet claiming ownership of pages that were renamed; regenerate it from routable content rather than maintaining it by hand. Why: With this much overlapping content, the fastest ranking win is stopping two of the site's own pages from fighting over one query. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Design Quick Win

Details

The badge detail page renders one header composition (image, name, rarity chip, status pill) whatever the badge's stage, so an upcoming badge, an open one and an extinct one all lead with the same weight. Design Quick Win

Scroll-spy category header in the grid

Details

Design Quick Win

Shared-element transition from tile to detail

Details

Clicking a tile hard-cuts to the detail page even though the same image is at both ends. Only the image and the title carry names, so the transition count stays small. Effort: M. Design Quick Win

Server-honest suspense reveal for game rounds

Details

The 13 games and the wheel are server-authoritative, so the client does not know the outcome until the response lands and any spin or flip is theatre of unknown length. Design the reveal to be honest about that: the wheel's easing is computed to land on the server's slot within the time the request actually takes, with a minimum spin so a fast response still reads as deliberate, and the games use a fixed-length reveal that continues while the response is in flight and resolves when it arrives, never announcing a result the client invented. The pending phase is designed suspense, not a spinner covering the board. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an animation that resolves client-side and then contradicts the server payload, or a reveal that overruns on a slow response and looks frozen exactly when the player is most invested. Why: For games whose whole promise is server-decided fairness, the reveal should look like it is waiting for the truth rather than inventing it. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Quick Win

Numeric and date presentation layer

Details

Numbers are formatted ad hoc across the site: owner counts, TBRI scores, coin balances, XP, percentages, dates and durations each have their own rounding and their alignment differs between a table, a tile and a chart label. Design Quick Win

Wrapping, hyphenation and measure policy

Details

Headings break mid-phrase, a long badge name orphans one word onto a third line, and prose runs the full width of a wide card. Add a typographic policy: text-wrap balance on headings and section titles, text-wrap pretty with widow control on lead paragraphs, hyphens auto with the correct lang per locale so long German and Russian badge names break sensibly, and a per-locale measure ceiling applied to .prose-content and the FAQ rather than inherited from the container width. This is about wrapping and measure specifically, not the type-scale and logical-inset work already on the board. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: hyphenation that splits a badge name into an unrecognisable fragment, or balanced headings that wrap into a taller and emptier block than the ragged one they replaced. Why: This site is mostly text about small images, and clean wrapping is most of what separates a reference page from a wall. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

The owner-trend chart on a badge page plots a bare line, but the events that explain it, the window opening, a mid-window re-drop, the closing date, are not on it. Design Quick Win

Linked crosshair across the stats dashboard

Details

The /stats page stacks a dozen independent charts, so reading what happened the week of the 14th means hovering the same date twelve times and holding twelve numbers in your head. Add a linked crosshair: hovering or focusing any chart publishes the resolved bucket, day or week, to a shared context, every other chart draws a thin rule at that bucket and shows its value in the chart head, and moving to another chart moves the whole column. The bucket is the shared key, so it works across different series with no new queries. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: a handler publishing on every pixel of movement and re-rendering twelve charts per frame, or a linked rule landing on a bucket another chart does not have and silently reading nothing. Why: The dashboard is a set of series over one time axis, and the only question worth asking of it is what several did at the same moment. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Badges Neutral

Silhouette Showdown

Details

A daily quiz game built from the real catalog art: the page renders a badge PNG as a pure-black silhouette (or a 12-pixel downsample) and asks the user to name the badge from four catalog options. Correct answers award XP and a streak multiplier; the pool is drawn from badges the user has *seen* in the catalog so the difficulty is personalized. Why it fits: The catalog already ships every badge image plus its title, category and rarity, so the entire game content is derived, not authored. Badges Neutral

Badge Futures Market

Details

A prediction market where users stake BadgesCoins on claims about the catalog: "badge X will cross 50,000 owners within 30 days", "badge Y will drop out of the legendary tier this month". Positions are priced by the crowd, settled automatically from the `badge_stats` time series, and the market history for each badge is shown on its detail page. Why it fits: The 15-minute potat sync already writes owner-count time series per badge and recomputes rarity (TBRI) each run, so every market is objectively resolvable without a human oracle — the exact precondition a prediction market needs. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: If settlement windows are generous and oracle rules ambiguous, a handful of users with scripted potat reads can farm near-certain markets and drain the coin supply. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Aufwand

Solide Idee, aber nicht dringend. Aufwand (3/5) und Nutzen (4/5) sind ausgeglichen.

Badges Neutral

Badge Time Machine

Details

A global, scrubable timeline of the entire catalog: drag a slider to any past date and the badge grid, owner counts, rarity tiers and "claimable now" state re-render as they were on that day. A "then vs now" mode overlays two dates so you can see exactly which badges were added, expired or demoted in between. Why it fits: Snapshot history from the 15-minute potat run plus every catalog diff already recorded in the changelog is a full event log; the time machine is a read-only projection over data that currently only powers the trend chart on one page. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: Point-in-time reconstruction from a sparse series will interpolate silently and present invented numbers as fact — every cell needs an explicit "as of" timestamp and a staleness marker. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Badges Neutral

Leak Radar

Details

A dedicated page that lists badges detected in the catalog feed *before* Twitch announces them — inserted via the badgebase sync's upcoming path — with a per-badge "spotted N hours before official release" counter and a confidence note on the source. A push topic lets people subscribe only to leaks, separate from the normal new-badge push. Why it fits: The badgebase sync deliberately inserts upcoming badges ahead of their live window by matching image UUIDs; that early-warning side effect is currently invisible. Surfacing it turns an implementation detail of the sync into a genuinely distinctive feature no badge site offers. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: If "leak" is used loosely, it becomes rumor-mongering — one false positive published as a leak destroys the credibility of the whole page, so the detection threshold must be conservative and sourced. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Badges Neutral

Fading Badges

Details

A leaderboard and per-badge status built on the gap between *owned* and *still displayed*: a badge whose owner count is flat while its wear share craters is flagged "fading", a badge with both rising is "rising". Each badge gets a small owned-vs-worn divergence chart with a "worn by X% of owners today" line. Why it fits: TBRI already computes wear as 15% of the score, but the site only ever shows the resulting number, never the divergence itself. Making the two inputs visible turns an internal rarity component into a browsable signal — "badges people regret claiming" is a story the data already holds. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: Wear share is a single-source measurement (potat), so an outage looks like a mass abandonment event; the page needs to mark low-confidence windows rather than declaring every badge "fading". Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Badges Neutral

Drop Survival Lab

Details

A statistics page that treats every claim window as a survival observation and renders Kaplan-Meier curves of "how long badges stay claimable", split by category, free-vs-paid and year. A live table shows the median remaining claim time for badges currently open, so collectors can see which windows historically close fast. Why it fits: The badgebase sync stores start and end for every drop window (`data-reset` dates); that is a complete survival dataset nobody has ever analyzed. It gives the catalog a genuine quantitative-research artifact instead of another grid. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: Windows that are still open are right-censored data; treating them as completed (or dropping them) biases every curve, so the estimator must handle censoring correctly or the numbers are quietly wrong. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Badges Neutral

Achievement Composer

Details

A page where users write their own achievement as a predicate over catalog fields — "own every badge from category X released in 2024", "own 3 badges in the legendary tier" — and the self-evaluating achievement engine awards it when satisfied. Composed achievements appear on the profile next to the 125 built-in ones and are shareable by URL. Why it fits: Achievements are already self-evaluating rather than hard-coded checks, so a user-authored predicate is an extension of the existing evaluator, and the catalog fields (category, tier, release date, owned) are exactly the vocabulary users want to write against. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: User-expressed predicates are a query language, and a naive evaluator is both a DoS vector and a way to write impossible goals — the vocabulary must be a fixed allow-listed schema, never translated SQL. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Badges Neutral

TBRI-100 — a market index for the rarest badges

Details

A single charted index: the mean TBRI of the 100 rarest badges, rebased to 100 at launch, updated with each potat sync. A badge page states whether that badge is "outperforming the index" and a `/index` page shows the line with the biggest movers of the day. Why it fits: rarity inputs and their history already land in `badge_stats`; the index is one aggregate query and one Recharts chart. Collectors get the language of a market ("my collection beat the index this month") without any real-money implication. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a moving index invites day-trading impulses; it must read as a curiosity, not as a score to defend, and never gate any reward. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-02.md

Badges Neutral

Details

A single number on the profile derived from rarity and drop type — each owned badge contributes a TBRI-weighted value into a "collection worth," with a public net-worth board alongside the existing XP leaderboard and a breakdown showing which badges drive it. Badges Neutral

Window deadline change log

Details

Record every observed start and end date per window at each sync, so any change becomes a dated event on the badge page ('end moved from 12 Sep to 19 Sep') with the previous value shown, and a catalog filter for badges whose deadline has moved this month. The same history scores providers on how often their first listed end date matched the observed expiry, per category. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: scoring on any reported date rather than the first observed one makes a provider that posts honest corrections look inaccurate, which is the opposite of the truth. Why: A deadline that moves is the most common way a collector misses a badge, and nothing records the movement today. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Neutral

Archived owner-series backfill

Details

A recovery pass pulls dated public snapshots of badge owner pages (Wayback captures of badgebase and potat, plus any other public archives) and inserts the recovered date and owner count into badge_stats marked as source 'archive', with the marker visible on the owner chart. This extends owner curves years earlier than this site has been collecting, so age, rarity and momentum rest on more than a few weeks of observations. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: archive captures are inconsistent and sometimes wrong, so an 'archived' point must never be allowed to overwrite a measured one and the chart must separate the two. Why: A badge's earliest weeks decide its rarity, and that is exactly the data a young database does not have. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Versioned rarity methodology page

Details

Publish the TBRI formula exactly as implemented, with one real badge's numbers worked through it step by step, the formula version, and the change history of that version so a score from six months ago can still be explained. The numbers are computed live from a stable badge through the same module the syncs use, never hard-coded into the page. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a worked example with hard-coded values breaks silently as the catalog changes, so it must be generated live and covered by a test that fails when the page and the formula disagree. Badges Neutral

Ghost median-curve overlay

Details

Draw a badge's owner curve against a ghost line: the median curve of comparable badges in the same category at a similar release age, built by aligning every series at age zero and taking the median per age bucket. The chart then reads ahead of the typical TwitchCon badge instead of a bare number, and the gap at 30 and 90 days becomes a comparable score. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the baseline mixes badges with different window lengths and provider coverage, so a minimum cohort size must be enforced and shown or the ghost line misleads. Why: An owner count means nothing without a comparison, and the catalog already holds thousands of comparable curves. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Cross-script search matching

Details

Index a transliterated form of every catalog name so a query typed in Japanese, Arabic or Cyrillic script, or in Latin transliteration of a non-Latin name, still finds the badge, and show in the result which form matched. Built as an additional index beside the existing search, never replacing it. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: aggressive transliteration creates false matches on short names, so results must display the matched form so a wrong hit is obvious immediately. Why: Gateway and ゲートウェイ are the same badge and the catalog currently answers only one of them. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Details

Rare badge combination detector

Details

Mine synced inventories for pairs and triples of badges that almost never co-occur because their windows were years apart or their audiences differ, and publish the hardest combinations with the few members who hold them (opt-in only). It is the inverse of recommendations: not what to chase next, but which combinations are nearly impossible. Effort: L. Badges Neutral

Status contradiction watch

Details

A nightly check flags badges whose stored status contradicts the evidence: marked active but unlisted by every provider for N days, or window open but zero new owners across M consecutive syncs, or end date long past with status still active. Each flag shows a small 'status uncertain' mark on the badge with the evidence sentence attached and lands in an admin queue. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a genuinely quiet badge in a quiet week is not broken, so the check must require both provider silence and owner stalling before it raises a flag. Why: A wrong status is worse than an unknown one, because a countdown reads as a promise. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Measured vs estimated owner series

Details

Uses the sync timestamp recorded on each badge_stats row. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: interpolation that looks like data is how a chart lies, so estimated spans must be visually distinct and excluded from any slope-based statistic. Catalog scope and exclusions page

Details

A public page documenting what this database deliberately leaves out: the status and role badge classes (moderator, VIP, partner, prime, turbo, staff and their siblings) with live counts pulled from Helix, the reason each is not a collectible catalog entry, and a read-only reference gallery of their current art so the many visitors who come looking for mod or VIP badge art find it and understand the boundary. The exclusion rule is derived from the same check the sync uses, so the page can never disagree with what is stored. Effort: S. Badges Neutral

Unknown-deadline honesty state

Details

Badges with no listed end date get an explicit 'no known deadline' state instead of an implied permanent one: no countdown, no urgency styling, a note that the deadline is unknown with the date we last looked, and a slot in an admin research queue of badges whose deadline is missing. Each badge records whether its deadline is listed, inferred from past windows, or unknown. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: silently treating unknown as permanent gives false safety on a badge that may close tomorrow, so the state must be visibly different from an evergreen badge. Why: An unknown deadline rendered as no deadline is the catalog's most expensive small lie. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Design Neutral

Touch target and hit-area policy

Details

The small controls here, the tier chip, a table row action, rail arrows, the countdown copy affordance, are visually 20 to 28 px, below a comfortable thumb target, so touches land on the row instead of the control. Define a hit-area policy: every interactive control guarantees a 44 px minimum target through an invisible padding or pseudo-element that does not change its visual size, controls in a tight row keep an 8 px minimum gutter so their expanded areas cannot overlap, and the policy is expressed once in the component classes. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: expanded areas overlapping until the neighbour is untappable, or a padding trick that breaks the alignment of a tight chip row. Why: The smallest controls on the site are also the most-used ones on the device where they are hardest to hit. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Spiele Neutral

Rarity Plinko Drop

Details

A ball drops through a peg pyramid whose bin payouts mirror the six rarity tiers, and the server precomputes reachable paths from a per-round seed so the distribution can be published and independently checked. The player chooses the bin width and risk band before the drop, and settlement uses the seeded path with the seed stored for later re-derivation. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a physics implementation the server does not own lets a client pick its own landing bin, so the drop must be a seeded lookup rather than a client simulation. Why: It is a household-name arcade game whose payout bins are the site's own rarity ladder. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Badge Derby Betting

Details

Eight catalog badges enter a simulated race whose lane speeds are drawn from the round seed but weighted by each badge's current TBRI, so rare badges start as favourites and the odds are printed before betting closes. Stakes go into a pari-mutuel pool, winners divide it after a published house cut, and the field, seed and pool are all stored for audit. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: one large late bet can drain a pool, so a last-thirty-second wager cap is needed, and a race whose result is not seeded is unverifiable. Why: It is a betting game whose form guide is the rarity index the site already publishes. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Badge Mahjong Exhibit

Details

Badge tiles are stacked in layers and a tile is playable only when both long edges are free, which makes the puzzle about uncovering artwork rather than memory. Matching pairs are same-set badges, and each pair both matched and revealed shows a one-line catalog fact. Boards are seeded per day and the server validates the submitted removal order before paying. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a generated layout can be unsolvable, so boards must be produced by reverse-dealing from a finished state and tested before they are published. Why: It is a familiar, calm tile game that pays out in catalog knowledge as well as coins. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Daily Shared Seed Ladder

Details

Every day each game gets one committed seed, and any round played under that seed is grouped into a daily ladder for that game, so members compare runs on an identical sequence rather than on luck. The ladder is a view over game_rounds filtered by the day's seed, and the seed is revealed with a hash at 00:00 UTC so the sequence could not have been chosen after seeing the plays. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a published seed lets a script pre-solve the sequence, so ladder rounds must be capped per member and the seed must be committed before it becomes usable. Why: It creates a fair daily competition where luck cannot explain away the winner. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Add-ons Neutral

Prestige track past level 100

Details

Past level 100 the XP bar does not stop: total XP beyond the 254,900 cap converts into prestige stars (one per 50,000 XP), shown as a small laurel count beside the level badge and in place of an unbounded level number on the leaderboards. Prestige is display-only, cannot be bought, and the level badge keeps its Mythic theme at zero stars. It reads the XP already stored in user_progress, so no new data source is needed. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: if the star threshold is too low the top of the ladder flattens within a week, so the spacing should be set from the observed XP distribution of the current top 100 before launch. Why: The level ladder ends at 100, and the site's longest-running players need somewhere for the number to keep meaning something. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Coin-priced in-round hints

Details

During a skill round a member can buy one hint through the normal server path: reveal a memory tile, remove two wrong quiz options, or show the vault's next digit, priced per game in the settings document and scaled to the current bet. The hint is consumed server-side in the same round, the round is flagged as hinted, and the payout is reduced by a fixed fraction so the option assists rather than pays. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: hints that leave the payout untouched make the skill games trivially profitable, so the payout reduction and the one-hint-per-round rule are the whole safety mechanism and must live in the engine, never the client. Why: The skill games have a hard wall for new players, and a priced hint converts frustration into a small, honest coin sink. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Bankroll coach bet suggestion

Details

Before a round a single line suggests a stake for the member's stated goal - 'you hold 12,000 coins and a 30,000 target; a flat 1 to 2 percent stake survives typical variance, an all-in does not' - computed on the server from the balance, that game's payout distribution constants and its configured limits. The suggestion is never auto-applied and can be dismissed permanently from arcade preferences. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a suggestion that reads as financial advice invites blame when it loses, so it must be framed as a variance note with the arithmetic shown and must reference only the site's own coins. Why: The arcade has a strong pull toward all-in play and the payout maths needed to counter it is already in the engine. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Seasonal arcade pass

Details

Each season carries a pass bought with BadgesCoins, never cash, unlocking a two-track reward path: a free track everyone fills by playing, and a paid track with table themes, a season profile frame and scrip. Progress is season XP earned from rounds, heists and the wheel, tracked per pass and reset with the season, and the pass grants no coins or XP multipliers so it cannot feed the economy. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: a pass is the classic grind machine, so the path must be short and reachable in a few sessions, and the paid track must never accept real money or the addon becomes commerce. Add-ons Neutral

Arcade scrip currency

Details

A second, non-purchasable currency is earned only by playing - a little per round, more for win streaks - and is spent on a second chance at a lost round, a bonus spin voucher, or arcade cosmetics. Scrip can never be converted into coins and is never granted for spending coins, so it rewards play without inflating the coin economy, and its balance is shown separately everywhere coins appear. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: scrip that converts to coins, directly or through a purchasable item, becomes a coin faucet, so the no-conversion rule must be enforced in the database, not only in application code. Why: A pure reward layer with no cash-out lets the arcade pay out generously for play while leaving the coin economy untouched. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Details

Per locale, compute the mean number of distinct paths a visitor touches in one session and the share of single-path sessions, then rank locales. A high-depth locale is reading the catalog; a high single-path share suggests people land and leave. Reads the analytics table columns locale, path and visitor hash. Effort: S. Profil Neutral

Collector Milestone Spine

Details

An automatic permanent chronicle built from rows the site already has: join date, first synced badge, each level bracket crossed in user_progress, each tier gain, first legendary owned, longest streak. Every entry carries the catalog context at that moment — the catalog size then, the badge's owner count then — so it reads as a story rather than a dump, and it lives at a stable profile sub-page. It is an event chronicle, not an inventory diff: there is no scrubber and no reconstruction of past ownership state. Effort: M. Guest Showcase Slots

Details

A member can lend one of their six slots to another member's badge: the tile renders as usual but attributed ('@name's badge, shown by consent'), and the guest can withdraw from their own profile at any time. Both sides opt in — the owner invites, the guest accepts — and the guest's ownership is re-checked against their latest sync before the tile renders. Details

A visitor can mark one tile on a profile per day as admired — no text, no score. Marks aggregate per tile into a small count and drive a 'most admired here' line above the shelves; the owner sees the daily curve of marks on their own profile, and a visitor's own profile lists how many collections they have admired. Profil Neutral

Collection Review Window

Details

A member opens a 72-hour window in which others submit a rubric score for the collection on three axes — breadth, rarity, curation — each with a required one-line justification; when it closes, the owner gets a digest with the median per axis, the strongest and weakest line, and reviewers attributed only if they opted in. One review per reviewer per window, and windows are capped to a few per year so the digest stays worth reading. It reviews a collection at a moment, unlike a standing endorsement of a person. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the justifications are unmoderated free text and repeated windows turn into a popularity contest, so both need caps and a report path. Why: Collectors want a real second opinion on their haul, and a time-boxed review is a stronger ritual than an endless comment box. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Category Completion Race

Details

Two members opt into a race over one category, each choosing a target from badges both of them lack, and the site tracks who syncs the missing badges first on a shared page with two progress bars and days elapsed. The race ends when one side completes the target, both profiles record the result as a friendly head-to-head, and a member can hold only one active race. Shared Coincidence Moments

Details

After each sync the site looks for pairs of members whose new inventory rows contain the same badge inside the same claim window, and offers both a shared 'we both claimed this' card with the badge, the window and the two names. Details

A member starts a relay by naming a badge and passing it on; the next member adds one related badge — same set, same era, or a deliberate contrast — with a one-line reason, then passes it again. The chain lives at its own URL showing every hop with attribution, and profiles list the chains a member contributed to. Help Exchange Ledger

Details

A member posts a specific small request — 'can someone in Brazil confirm this window is open', 'which of these two should I claim first' — and another fulfils it with a short answer; the fulfilment is logged as a completed exchange attributed to both. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it becomes a second comment section, so hard limits — one open request per member, no nested replies inside a fulfilment — must be enforced. Synchronised Claim Pools

Details

Members join a pool for a specific badge and hour; the site reminds everyone at the time and, after the next potat sync, publishes the measured owner-count step for that badge during the pool hour plus the pool's contribution ('137 owners in one hour'). The deliverable is evidence — a coordinated claim becomes a recorded catalog event with a chart, not just an RSVP list. Pool pages keep the badge's window, the participant count and the resulting chart as a small public record. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the pool claims credit for organic claims landing in the same hour, so the measurement must compare against the badge's own rolling hourly baseline rather than against zero. Details

Members invite others and both ends of the invite are recorded, producing a visible generation tree that shows how the community grew; an invitee starts with a small welcome grant and the inviter earns a purely cosmetic mark for each generation that stays active. The lattice is browsable ('see my branch'), every node is a normal profile, and an invite cannot be reattributed after the fact. Effort: M. Profil Neutral

Streamer Channel Link

Details

A member who streams can link their Twitch channel to their profile, which then shows a cached live-or-offline pill, a pinned note they write for their viewers, and the badges currently claimable so a viewer can act while watching. The pill resolves through a five-minute cache — one Helix stream lookup per channel per window, well inside the existing hourly budget — and linking requires re-authenticating with Twitch so nobody can claim someone else's channel. The link is part of the public profile, not a separate widget page. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: live lookups blow past the rate budget on popular channels, so the cache window and a per-channel lock are the guard. Why: Streamers and their viewers are already the site's audience, and the profile is where that relationship should resolve. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Profil Neutral

Vouch Chain Provenance

Details

An established member can vouch for a newer one, and the vouch appears on the newcomer's profile as a chain ('vouched by @a, who was vouched by @b'). A vouch unlocks a small set of social surfaces — guestbook stamps, relay hops, race entry — for accounts too new to qualify on their own; vouches are one-way, attributed, revocable with a reason, and an established member may hold only a few live at once. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: vouch farming as a growth hack, so the small per-voucher limit plus revocation and the visible chain keep it costly. Why: New members cannot use the social layer until someone trusts them, and a visible chain makes that trust a real commitment. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Commitment Reliability Record

Details

A profile-facing log of the social commitments a member accepted and completed — races finished, relays continued, review windows honoured, introductions taken up — with abandoned ones listed too. It is a plain dated list with outcomes, deliberately not a single score, and it gates nothing: visibility is the only consequence. Annual Community Awards

Details

Once a year members nominate and vote in a small fixed set of categories — most helpful, best curator, best data catch, friendliest newcomer — and winners carry a dated award line on their profile permanently, with the nomination text quoted. The ballot is capped at one nomination per category per member, votes are per account, and results publish only after a reveal date so interim tallies cannot be watched. It is the community rewarding behaviour rather than data ranking collectors. Effort: M. Profil Neutral

Open House Windows

Details

A member can schedule a window — announced up to a week ahead, lasting 24 or 48 hours — during which their inventory is public and then closes itself; the profile shows a countdown and a visitor counter that resets each time. Because it is time-based rather than a standing preference, a member can show off for a weekend without going permanently public, and visitors see a normal collection during the window and the usual notice after it. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a forgotten window leaves an inventory public indefinitely, so closure must be enforced at query time from the timestamp, never by a cron that can fail. Why: It gives private collectors a safe, bounded way to be seen, which the current binary setting cannot. Source: new-ideas-07-profile.json

Performance Neutral

Take third-party calls out of the render

Details

/badges/[slug] calls fetchBadgeLiveStats(set_id) against potat during render and /profile/[username] calls fetchUserBadges() when the inventory looks stale, so a third party's latency becomes the page's latency. Both values are already written by the 15-minute potat sync and by the inventory sync, so read the stored columns during render and let the syncs own every outbound call. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the owner counter becomes only as fresh as the last sync, so keep the as-of timestamp the badge page already has room for, or a stale number reads as live. Why: The site already syncs these values on a schedule, so making a visitor's page wait on the upstream provider is cost with no freshness gain. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Neutral

Cache badge art in the service worker

Details

public/sw.js handles push only, so every repeat visit re-downloads the same badge PNGs from static-cdn.jtvnw.net along with the versioned Next static chunks. Add a fetch handler that serves badge art and /_next/static/ cache-first, which is safe because the badge URL already carries set_id and version. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a cache-first handler in front of the HTML shell serves a stale page forever, so match only the image hosts and the versioned static path and leave navigations network-first. Why: Badge art is immutable per version and is the site's dominant asset volume, so caching it on the device removes most repeat-visit network cost. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Neutral

A two-speed potat sync

Details

The every-15-minutes GitHub Actions job hits /api/cron/potat, which runs the full distribution and owner sweep across the whole catalog, roughly 476 badges, on every tick. Split it: a cheap pass that only asks about badges whose window is open or whose count moved runs on the 15-minute cadence, while the full reconciliation stays in the daily 06:30 cron. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a changed-only pass that trusts its own last state drifts from reality after any provider gap, so keep the daily full sweep authoritative and make the frequent pass strictly additive. Why: Ninety-six full-catalog sweeps a day is the site's largest recurring external cost, and most of those runs find nothing new. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Neutral

A per-route query-count budget in CI

Details

Nothing notices when a page quietly gains a round trip, which is how /badges grew its extra reads in the first place. Add a script that renders a fixed list of routes against the seeded local Supabase, counts the PostgREST calls per route, and fails when a route exceeds its recorded budget (/badges at most 3, badge detail at most 5, /stats at most 6). Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a hard-coded budget becomes a number nobody wants to raise and blocks a legitimate new section, so store the budget per route with its reason and require the raise to come with an explanation. Why: Round-trip count is the cheapest metric that predicts this site's response time, and it is the only one that can be asserted in a build. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Performance Neutral

Share third-party rankings across instances

Details

fetchBadgesBlogRanking holds a module-level Map with a six-hour TTL that dies with each serverless instance, so a cold instance re-fetches badges.blog/api/ranking during a /leaderboards render, and each of the 11 locales can pay it again. Persist the ranking in a small table or an unstable_cache keyed without the locale, written by the sync, so one copy serves every instance and language. Effort: M. Bedienbarkeit Neutral

One formatter module with a lint rule

Details

Audits keep finding fixed-decimal calls, argument-less locale string calls that format in the server's time zone, and hand-built number strings across the pages. Centralise number, compact, percent, date and duration formatting in one module that always takes a locale and an explicit time zone, and add a lint rule that fails on the banned call shapes outside that module, then migrate the call sites and delete the duplicates (the compact formatter currently lives inside a component). Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a mechanical sweep changes rendered strings and can break anything asserting on text, and a lint rule that fires on legitimate non-display uses gets disabled wholesale. Why: Eleven locales and a shared number formatter is the cheapest structural fix for a whole class of presentation bugs. Source: new-ideas-09-usability.json

Content Neutral

Hreflang and canonical audit

Details

A check over the eleven locale trees verifying that each page canonicalises to itself, emits hreflang alternates for all locales plus x-default, never points two locales at one canonical, and never lets the locale prefix drop out of a canonical or a link. A scheduled walk over a fixed list of route templates reads each rendered head and reports missing, duplicated or self-conflicting tags, and flags any page whose translated copy is byte-identical to English (an untranslated page that probably should not be indexed yet). Risk if built badly: a canonical mistake de-indexes eleven locales at once, so run the audit read-only first and treat any canonical change as a reviewed edit rather than an automated fix. Why: Eleven locales multiply the content surface elevenfold and also multiply the ways a single wrong canonical can erase it. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Details
