Design Neutral

Return-position contract for the catalog

Details

Leaving the catalog for a badge and coming back loses the visitor's place: the grid re-renders from the top and the tile they opened is unmarked. Define a return-position contract that persists the scroll offset and the focused tile id per route in sessionStorage, restores them on back navigation, and paints a brief you-were-here ring on the tile that was opened, cleared on the next interaction. The state is client-only and survives the App Router remount. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: restoring a stale offset after the grid contents changed, dropping the visitor into the middle of a different list or onto a tile that no longer exists. Why: Catalog browsing is inherently exploratory, and punishing every dive into a badge with a lost position is a tax on the main activity. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Neutral

FLIP choreography for badges that arrive live

Details

The catalog re-renders when the sync adds badges, but new tiles appear by reflowing the grid and yanking whatever the visitor is reading. Give the grid a defined insert behaviour: new tiles animate in on entry while existing tiles FLIP to their new positions, and a compact N new badges pill appears above the grid instead of auto-scrolling, so the visitor chooses when to jump. Positions come from getBoundingClientRect before and after the render, gated behind the motion preference and capped so a bulk sync does not animate a hundred tiles. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an animation on every revalidation that makes a stable grid feel unstable, or measurement work that forces a layout thrash on a large page. Why: This is a real-time database, so the grid changes under people's hands and that should feel like an update rather than a glitch. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Neutral

Coin-flight arcs from payout to HUD

Details

When a game, the wheel or the daily login pays out, the HUD balance simply changes with no link between cause and effect. Choreograph the payment instead: the result panel emits a short arc of coin glyphs that fly from the payout element to the HUD coin counter, which ticks up as they land, with the path computed from the two elements' bounding boxes and the glyph count capped so a jackpot does not spawn hundreds of elements. The motion preference and the motion-level setting skip the flight and update the number directly. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: dozens of animated nodes on a big win, an arc that keeps running after the visitor navigates away, or a displayed balance that drifts from the server value it animates toward. Why: The economy is the part of the site people interact with most, and the payout is the moment worth animating. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Neutral

Data-semantic palette separate from rarity

Details

The six rarity tiers have ramps, but ordinary status, upcoming, open, closed, expired, stale and degraded, borrows the generic success/warning/danger tokens, so an expired badge reads as a failure in red and a stale catalog reads like an outage. Define a separate data-semantic palette for lifecycle and health that never uses the rarity hues and never uses danger red for a badge that simply ended: upcoming a neutral outline, open the accent, closed a muted fill, expired a desaturated neutral, and stale and degraded a warning tone of their own. Apply it to status pills, table rows, chart bands and the lifecycle glyphs together. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: collapsed semantics where open and the accent become indistinguishable, or red reserved so tightly that a genuine data failure stops standing out. Why: Rarity and lifecycle are two different stories told in colour on the same page, and today they share one alphabet. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Design Neutral

Haptic vocabulary for game and wheel outcomes

Details

On phones the games and the wheel give no tactile confirmation, so a loss and a win land identically. Define a small haptic vocabulary using the Vibration API: a short tap for a bet placed or a selection, a double tap for a win, a single longer pulse for a bust or loss, and a distinct pattern for a jackpot-scale outcome, fired only where the API exists, behind an explicit preference that defaults off, and never for passive events such as a feed item arriving. It mirrors the visual outcome vocabulary rather than inventing a second meaning. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: buzzing on every interaction until it becomes noise, vibration during passive browsing, or haptics firing for an outcome the server has not confirmed yet. Why: The games are the most tactile part of the site and the one place where the phone's own feedback channel is currently silent. Source: new-ideas-03-design.json

Badge knowledge profile

Details

The quiz and memory games already know which catalog badges a member answered correctly, so store that as a private knowledge profile per user (badge_id, correct, seen_at) and surface 'you can name 243 of 476 badges' with the categories you are weakest in. The quiz then samples adaptively - roughly 70% from weak categories and 30% from everything - so the game stays learnable instead of cycling the same easy art, and a study list of the badges the member keeps missing links into the catalog. It uses only the existing catalog rows and the round results the quiz engine already returns. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: if the profile is public it becomes a published list of what a member does not know, so it must be private by default with an explicit opt-in to show a knowledge score. Why: The badge quiz is the one game whose difficulty could be personal, and the catalog already holds exactly the data to make it so. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Arcade Grand Prix circuit

Details

A scheduled circuit runs one day per game; each day's rounds feed that day's points (payout above a floor plus a placement bonus) and the cumulative table crowns an all-rounder champion with a season trophy. Points come from game_rounds filtered to the day's game, so nothing new is collected, and a live circuit page shows today's game, the standings and the days remaining. It differs from the single-elimination weekly bracket and from the seasonal ladder reset because it measures breadth across all 13 games over a fixed schedule. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: one day per game overruns a weekly promise, so the schedule length must be a panel setting, and the scoring formula must stop one enormous lucky payout from deciding the whole circuit. Why: A badge catalog rewards breadth of collecting, and the arcade has no equivalent that rewards breadth of play. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Risk profiles per round

Details

Before a round the member picks Conservative, Standard or Degenerate; the engine applies a defined tweak to that round's payout curve - Conservative pays more often and less, Degenerate pays rarely and large - with expected value held constant, and the chosen profile is stored on the round. The published-odds page gains a per-profile column so the choice is auditable against the same constants the engine reads. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: if the profiles do not conserve expected value one becomes strictly best and the others are decoration, so the constants must satisfy the identity and be verified in a test rather than eyeballed. Why: Every game offers one volatility, and the choice between steady and swingy is the thing players actually care about. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Benford Conformity Test

Details

Apply Benford's law to the leading digits of badge owner counts and coin balances and report the chi-square deviation per data source, where a strong deviation flags fabricated, truncated or mis-parsed numbers. Reads owner counts from badges or badge_stats. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: narrow numeric ranges violate the premise of Benford's law, so the test must restrict to spans of at least three orders of magnitude. Why: A cheap, rigorous check that the site's core number has not been silently corrupted by a parsing bug. Source: new-ideas-06-stats.json

Reads badges, blog posts and view counts. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: detecting coverage by title string match is fuzzy, so posts must reference badges through an explicit payload field rather than free text. Why: Points content effort at the rare, sought-after badges that currently have no explanation anywhere. Source: new-ideas-06-stats.json

Badges Neutral

Timezone Drop Lottery

Details

Convert every drop and claim window to the visitor's local time and show a personal "windows you slept through" count, plus the badges you own that were only claimable between 02:00 and 06:00 your time. Why it fits: badgebase windows carry absolute start/end timestamps; intersecting them with a visitor's timezone offset is a subtraction. It turns global-window fairness into something a collector can finally see and joke about, and it explains their own gaps. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: timezone handling is the classic source of off-by-one claims ("you missed it") — windows must render in the user's zone with the zone named, never in server time. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-02.md

Content Neutral

Badge Soundtrack

Details

Turns a badge's six TBRI signals into a short looping audio clip — owner scarcity drives tempo, claim-window brevity drives rhythm density, age drives the octave. Each badge gets a play button next to its rarity score and a deterministic seed so the same badge always sounds the same. Why it fits: TBRI is the site's proprietary differentiator and the computation already exists in `rarity.ts`; a deterministic mapping to audio needs no new data, only a client-side synth. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: autoplay audio and accessibility regressions — must be click-to-play, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`, and never autostart. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-04.md

Badges Neutral

Second-source catalog probe

Details

Helix is the official badge source but is not complete: event, regional and legacy sets are missing from it. A low-frequency probe of the badge-sets endpoint the Twitch web client's own badge picker uses diffs against the Helix catalog and proposes missing rows with their art URL and set id, each flagged as a secondary source until the normal sync or a human confirms it. Proposed rows are excluded from feeds and from the rarity math until confirmed. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an undocumented internal endpoint can change or throttle without notice, and a probe that guesses too much pollutes the authoritative catalog, so results must stay proposals and never auto-insert. Why: Catalog completeness is the product, and the official API is provably not the entire catalog. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Neutral

Badge art pack export

Details

From a category, a set page or your own inventory, generate a downloadable pack: one sprite sheet of the selected art at 1x, 2x and 4x plus a JSON manifest mapping each tile to its badge id, title and source URL. Built for streamers, emote tools and Discord bots that otherwise re-download badge art file by file. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: redistributing Twitch art without attribution is a terms problem, so the manifest must carry the source URL and a usage note on every pack. Why: Everyone building a Twitch tool needs badge art in bulk and the catalog only offers single images. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Badges Neutral

Pixel-art crisp rendering policy

Details

Detect badges whose art is upscaled pixel art (blocky edges, small palette, nearest-neighbour scaling artefacts) and apply a per-badge rendering policy so those tiles and detail images scale with nearest-neighbour rather than smoothing, storing the flag alongside the badge. A catalog-wide 'crisp render' pass fixes blurry pixel badges at every size already displayed, with no new asset needed. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a wrong flag makes a smooth badge look jagged, so the detector needs a manual override and should only switch rendering where confidence is high. Why: Most Twitch badge art is pixel art and the site currently blurs all of it at the sizes people actually see. Source: new-ideas-01-badge.json

Content Neutral

Content performance attribution

Details

A report answering which written pages actually bring people into the catalog: aggregate the anonymous path sequences so a visit that passes through a post, tutorial or glossary entry and then reaches a badge detail page or a signup is credited to the entry page. It outputs assisted catalog visits and assisted signups beside raw views, so a lower-traffic article that converts well is visible next to a high-traffic one that does not. Data: the analytics table's path, visitor hash, timestamp and locale, aggregated across visitors only. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: attribution invites joining sequences to identity; keep it strictly aggregate, refuse to output any row below a minimum visitor count, and never expose a per-visitor path. Why: Without it the team optimizes for pageviews on a site whose actual goal is people reaching and claiming badges. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Spiele Neutral

Badge Hand Poker

Details

A round deals five cards from a 52-card deck where each card maps deterministically to a catalog badge, with suit taken from category and rank from TBRI tier plus release year. The player may discard and redraw once, then the server evaluates the hand against a fixed paytable and pays by stake. The mapping lives server-side and a fresh deck is shuffled per round from a stored seed. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: if the mapping is client-visible a player can pre-compute which hand is a royal flush and only play those rounds, so the deck, mapping and shuffle must all be server-owned. Why: It makes the catalog itself the deck, so a poker game here can never be badges pasted onto a generic table. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Odd One Out

Details

A timer starts and four badge tiles appear: three drawn from one set_id or category and one intruder pulled from a different set chosen so its artwork is deliberately similar. The player taps the intruder, and the server selects the trio and the intruder from the catalog, withholds the answer and scores only taps that arrive before the server-issued deadline, paying by remaining milliseconds. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: weak intruder selection turns every round into a coin flip, so similarity must be computed from image hashes or shared set attributes rather than random picks. Why: It is a fast, endlessly replayable use of badge artwork the site already stores, requiring no trivia lookup. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Mosaic Reveal Nonogram

Details

Each day the server picks one catalog badge and rasterises its PNG into a black-and-white pixel matrix, then derives a nonogram from that matrix; the player fills cells and the server validates the finished grid against the stored clue set before paying. Difficulty scales with grid size, and a correct solve reveals the badge art at full resolution for a small bonus. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: image processing on the request path is slow and repeatable, so the matrix and clues must be generated once by a nightly job and stored, never per visitor. Why: It turns real badge artwork into a real logic puzzle whose solution is the badge itself. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Claim Window Minesweeper

Details

A server-generated board hides claim windows as mines, and the digit hints count how many adjacent windows opened in the same month, so the numbers are genuine catalog facts rather than arbitrary. Clearing a board pays scaled by server-measured elapsed time and mine density, and the board seed is stored so a result can be re-derived. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: payouts that scale only with board size can be ground out by a script that knows the optimal sweep, so pay by elapsed time and cap the number of paid boards per day. Why: Its clues encode the drop calendar, which makes knowing the catalog genuinely useful rather than decorative. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Badge Brick Breaker

Details

A paddle bounces a ball into rows of badge bricks; bricks from the same set sit adjacent, and clearing a full set row drops a power-up named after that set's category. Server physics runs from the stored seed plus the submitted input timeline at a fixed timestep, and the payout is a function of bricks cleared and server-measured session length. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: verifying a long input timeline is expensive and pad-prone, so cap the payable session at sixty seconds and pay only for cleared bricks within it. Why: It uses set_id grouping as level design, which a generic breakout clone cannot replicate. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Badge Snake Run

Details

The snake may only eat badges whose claim window is currently open, so its growth is governed by the live claimable set the catalog already computes, and it starves if the set is empty. Walls and obstacles are laid out from the set's category layout, and a round ends after a fixed number of server ticks so scores are comparable. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a client-side loop is trivially slowed by throttling frames, so the server must own the tick count and accept only an input sequence. Why: It is the simplest arcade loop there is, wired to a feed that changes every day. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Keno Catalog Draw

Details

The board is thirty catalog badges, the player marks four to ten of them, and the server draws fifteen from a seeded draw. Payouts follow a published paytable keyed on how many marks were hit, and every draw stores its seed so a member can re-derive it. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: pick counts and paytables tuned by feel produce a return over 100 percent, so the paytable must be computed from exact hypergeometric odds at build time. Why: It is a draw game whose tokens are badges a member can recognise and already chases. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Spiele Neutral

Fleet of Sets

Details

A grid hides fleets built from set_ids, so a three-cell ship is a set with three badges, and the AI hunts the player's fleet with a probability map over remaining catalog rows. A hit reveals the badge that occupied the cell, giving the hunt a light memory flavour, and all board state and AI moves live on the server. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a hunt whose AI runs client-side can be read and countered, so the opponent and the board must never be separated across the network. Why: It converts set structure into a spatial hunt with real catalog rows as the payload. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Details

Instead of a fixed paytable, a round's payout scale is set by how its result ranks against every other qualifying round of the same game that day, so a top-decile result pays more than one that merely beat the house. The server computes the daily distribution from game_rounds and settles at the end of the UTC day, showing a live percentile band during play. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: peers can coordinate tiny qualifying stakes to inflate the pool, so qualifying rounds need a minimum stake and the pool must be house-funded rather than player-funded. Why: It makes a member's competition the other members rather than the machine, which is what a collector community wants. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

Details

Details

Auto-play with stop conditions

Details

In the games with no per-round decision (slots, scratch, fixed-bet roulette) a member starts an auto-run of up to N rounds at a fixed stake with stop conditions - stop on a win above X, stop after Y losses, stop below balance Z - paced by the engine's one-round-per-second limit and always cancellable between rounds. Every round still goes through the normal server path, so the audit trail and rate limit are unchanged. Effort: M. Performance Neutral

Ship the non-Latin OG font subsets

Details

/api/og/profile loads a Google Fonts subset per request for any non-Latin display name, so sharing a CJK or Arabic profile makes a cold instance call a third party before it can draw the card. Pre-subset the families it uses at build time, or fetch each full family into the bundle once, as static assets under public/, keeping the per-instance cache as a second layer. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a bundled subset that misses a glyph a name actually uses renders boxes for exactly the users the runtime fetch was added for, so keep that fetch as the fallback when the bundled subset does not cover the text. Why: Profile cards are the site's share surface, and today an Arabic or Japanese collector's card depends on an external font fetch succeeding during a crawler request. Source: new-ideas-08-performance.json

Feed Megaphone

Risk if built badly: the feed becomes an advertising wall, so the slot must be rate-limited, length-capped and moderated. Why: The feed is the site's one social surface, and selling a scarce slot on it is a coin sink that fits the site. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Risk if built badly: a naive linear fit over a non-linear claim curve produces confidently wrong "final rarity" numbers. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-06.md

Sonstiges Neutral

`SYNC_DRY_RUN=1` for every engine: print the diff, write nothing

Details

Give each engine a dry-run mode that runs the full snapshot → diff → plan pipeline and prints the intended inserts, updates, demotions and deletes as a table, then exits without a single write and without a changelog row. Make it the first thing a contributor runs after touching a sync, and document it in the README next to the `npm run sync:*` table. Destructive sweeps (the badgebase demotion, the global status-badge deletion) become reviewable before they are shipped rather than after. Effort: M. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-09.md

Sonstiges Neutral

One shared provider-fetch wrapper: timeout, classified errors, and a circuit breaker

Details

Today each provider has its own retry behaviour (potat honours `Retry-After`, others differ). Consolidate them behind a single `fetchJson` that applies a hard per-request timeout, classifies errors (network / 4xx-contract / 429 / 5xx), counts consecutive failures per provider, and opens a breaker for the remainder of the run once a provider is clearly down. The breaker state is written into the heartbeat so "we skipped badges.blog on purpose" is legible in the data rather than a mystery of missing rows. Effort: M. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-09.md

Aufwand

Sonstiges Neutral

Provider registry: adding a provider becomes one file, not an engine edit

Details

Define one interface a provider implements (`capabilities` — catalog / enrichment / owner stats / per-user ownership — plus a `fetch` and a `normalize`), and have the sync engines iterate a registry instead of calling providers by name. Each provider gets its own heartbeat entry, its own freshness SLO and its own breaker state for free. Adding a fifth source (or making a second source authoritative for one capability) then touches one new file, not the three engines, and the "which source wins" decision becomes data in the registry rather than an if-branch buried in a sync. Effort: L. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-09.md

Content Neutral

Structured-data coverage audit

Details

A report that walks every indexable route type and records which structured-data blocks it emits — HowTo on badge pages, FAQPage on the generated FAQs, Article on posts, DefinedTerm on glossary entries, BreadcrumbList, ItemList on landing pages — and which are missing or invalid for that route type, with counts and the specific rows failing. It validates a sample, so a regression in the render path is visible before search results degrade. Data: a route-type registry in code plus the catalog and content rows it enumerates. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it becomes a red dashboard nobody reads and its expectation table falls behind the pages; keep the expectation list next to the components that emit the markup. Why: Rich results are how a badge page wins a search click over the wiki, and they break silently the moment a component changes. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Neutral

Content maintenance queue

Details

A ranked list of published pages that need attention, scored from two signals the site already owns: the page's traffic decay, from the analytics table's per-path views over recent weeks, and fact drift, where a badge cited by the page has since expired, changed tier or left the catalog. Every row names the page, the reason it surfaced, the specific stale reference, and a one-click path to edit it. It runs after the syncs so the queue always reflects the catalog as it now is, and it excludes pages that legitimately never change. Data: analytics paths and views, badge rows and badge_events, and the references extracted from page bodies. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it floods editors with hundreds of low-value rows until the queue is ignored; cap it per week and rank by traffic at risk rather than raw staleness count. Why: Content rots fastest on a site whose subject changes daily, and a decay queue is the only way an archive stays true. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Neutral

Pre-publish fact-check gate

Details

Before a post or glossary entry publishes, a check extracts every number and badge reference from the body, compares owner counts, dates and tier claims against the live database, and shows the editor a diff: what the draft claims versus what the catalog says today. The publish button stamps an as-of date by default so a number is never presented as permanently true, and the editor can pin a claim to a snapshot when the intent is deliberately historical. Data: the draft body, the referenced badge rows, and badge_stats for the cited metric. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the extractor misfires on incidental numbers and trains editors to click through blindly; hard-block only sequential claims such as owner counts and dates, and warn on everything else. Why: A badge database publishing a written claim about its own data is the one place a wrong number is unforgivable. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Neutral

AI-answer readiness pack

Details

A machine-readable, human-edited front door for answer engines: an llms.txt-style summary saying what the site is, what it tracks and where the data comes from, plus a small set of concise citable fact pages — catalog size, how rarity is defined, what is dropping this week, the biggest rarity movers — written as short prose with explicit as-of dates and stable anchors. Each fact page is regenerated from live data and carries a suggested citation line. Data: stats views and the current catalog for the numbers, authored prose for the framing. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: the summarized facts go stale and an assistant quotes the site's own outdated page back at users; regenerate numbers on each read and date every block. Why: When someone asks an assistant which badges exist, the site either supplies the citable answer or is paraphrased from a worse source. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Neutral

Collection-aware newsletter sections

Details

The digest is assembled from fixed sections — new drops, your categories, deadline warnings, rarity movers, new writing — and their order is chosen per subscriber from their own synced inventory, so someone who owns only Prime badges reads that section first and a category they own nothing in last. It reuses the existing send path and the inventory the potat sync already stores, computes the order at send time, and falls back to the default order when a subscriber has no synced badges. Data: per-user inventory, catalog category mix, closing drop windows, subscriber record. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the reordering is so aggressive that a subscriber never sees a new category and stops discovering anything; keep every section present and change only the order. Why: The site already knows what each subscriber collects, and a digest that ignores that is a generic mailing list. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Neutral

Editorial revision history with diffs

Details

Every revision of a post, glossary entry or tutorial is stored as a versioned row (body, editor, timestamp, note), and each published page gains a quiet last-updated line with a diff view between any two versions. Editors can see who changed a fact and when, and a reader can see that a page citing owner numbers was actually re-checked, which is the difference between a decaying blog and a living reference. Data: content rows, an author relation, and the existing audit log for attribution. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the diff exposes an unpublished draft or an internal note; scope diffs to published versions only and keep edit notes internal unless explicitly published. Why: On a data site, a page with a visible revision history reads as maintained, and a page without one reads as abandoned. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Content Neutral

Data-story investigations

Details

Occasional long-form analysis pieces that start from a question the catalog can actually answer and answer it with charts and a stated method: whether claim windows have shortened over the years, which categories have grown rarest, how one badge family's owner counts moved version by version. Each piece publishes its query, its sample size and the date the data was pulled so a reader can check the work, and its charts come from the same stats views the dashboard uses. Data: badge_stats series, badge_events window history, catalog fields. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: the analysis over-reads a third-party owner estimate as ground truth; every piece must state the margin of the underlying data and avoid causal claims about why a window moved. Why: Nobody publishes badge data journalism, so the site that holds the time series can own the definitive analysis pieces. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Translator termbase

Details

A shared termbase for the eleven locales holding the tokens that must never be translated (Prime Gaming, TwitchCon, Hype Train, Turbo, badge set names, tier names) with their approved form per locale, plus the approved translation of recurring UI vocabulary so claimable is the same word in every file. A check runs over a draft or a message file and flags a term used outside its approved form or a do-not-translate token that was mutated. Data: a termbase table keyed by term and locale, message catalogs and editorial drafts to check. Effort: S. Event landing pages

Details

A curated landing page per recurring real-world moment — each TwitchCon, each anniversary of a landmark drop, an opening hype period — gathering the badges associated with it, the writing done around it and a live view of what is claimable during it. Unlike a generated landing page each one is introduced by an editor with a note on what to watch this year, and each is stamped with its own dates so an old event's page reads as archival rather than current. Data: badges tied to the event by date range and category, related posts, live drop windows. Effort: M. Add-ons Neutral

Per-game mastery tracks

Details

Each of the 13 games gets its own mastery level, computed from that member's game_rounds rows for that game - rounds played, rounds won, best payout, and a shrinking weight on the oldest rounds so mastery reflects current form. The mastery level and title (Novice, Adept, Veteran, Master) render on the game tile in the hub and on a per-game mastery board beside the existing global one, and a mastery-up writes the usual feed entry and changelog row. This is distinct from the global XP level and the coverage-based Curator Level, both of which ignore which games a member actually plays. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: weighting by bet size turns mastery into a wealth score, so the formula must count rounds and outcomes rather than coins wagered, or a single whale outranks genuine players. Why: The site has 13 games but no way to show that someone is genuinely good at one of them rather than merely high level. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Self-authored arcade contracts

Details

Before playing, a member writes a contract from a closed vocabulary - 'win 5 rounds of Tower of Badges at a bet of 100 or more within 7 days' - escrowing a coin deposit; the contract stores its window and is evaluated by the same server-side predicate style the achievements already use. Completing it returns the deposit plus a bonus scaled by difficulty, letting it expire returns the deposit minus a small fee, and a 'contracts written / kept' count sits on the profile. This differs from the server-issued daily trials and the fixed 125 achievements because the player sets the terms and stakes coins on them. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the clause vocabulary must be closed and validated server-side, or a member words an impossible clause, keeps the bonus, and the contract system mints coins. Why: Collectors already set their own goals in the catalog; giving the arcade the same self-directed structure turns play into a personal commitment. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Persistent rivalry ledger

Details

Two members declare a rivalry, and from then on every shared arena is scored into a persistent head-to-head record: rounds compared by net coins in the same game on the same day, heists between the pair, and wheel results, with wins, losses, biggest single margin and longest run. The rivalry card lives on both profiles behind a link only the pair can open, and either side can end it at any time. This is a standing record over time, unlike a duel's one-off best-of-three or the pairwise catalog compare. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: scoring must be computed from a cached daily aggregate rather than per page load, or the ledger runs a recompute over game_rounds and steal_attempts on every profile view. Why: Stealing and games already produce friction between members, and a rivalry ledger gives that friction a running score instead of a one-off event. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Spectator side-bets on rounds

Details

On a broadcast round a watcher places a small stake, capped well below the game's own maximum, on the next round's outcome before it resolves; the stake is escrowed server-side, settled when the round lands, and the watcher's net goes through the normal coin path. Bets void if the caster stops, and the caster earns a small rake so being watched is worth something. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: this is a pure-chance betting surface, so the cap, the per-watcher daily limit and the rake belong in the settings document rather than in code, and settlement must happen only after the round is committed server-side or a spectator can bet on a known result. Why: Watching is currently worth nothing and costs nothing; a capped stake plus a rake to the player turns spectators into participants. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Community strategy playbooks

Details

Each game gets a guide page where members publish short, attributed strategies ('cash out Tower at floor 4 - the expected value peaks there'), versioned and votable with the top-rated one pinned. Guides are length-capped plain text with a report control, votes are one per member per guide, and every guide records the engine-constants revision it was written against so the page can mark it stale after a rules change. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it is user text, so escaping and a report path are required from day one, and without the constants revision every balance change silently turns the top guide into bad advice. Why: Nothing on the site tells a new player how a game is actually played well, and the arcade's regulars already know. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Hall of Fame record book

Details

A nightly job reads game_rounds and steal_attempts to maintain an all-time record book: biggest single payout, longest coin-flip ladder, longest win streak, biggest heist, best day by net, longest wheel dry streak. Each record shows the holder, the value and the date it was set, with a 'beaten' entry when it falls; records live in their own small table so a holder keeps the title even after the underlying rows are pruned. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: recomputing extremes over a growing game_rounds table on every page load will not hold, so the nightly job must write a standing record and replace it only on a strictly better value. Why: Live leaderboards show who is ahead this week; a record book is the thing that makes an old result permanent. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

In-game round tape

Details

Inside each game panel a strip shows the last 20 rounds of that game for the member - bet, outcome, payout, net and a sparkline of running net - flagging any round that was hinted or insured. The rows are already in game_rounds; the tape is the per-round record the aggregate counters throw away. Selecting a row opens the round detail with its inputs and result payload. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: it must read only the signed-in member's rows, or a round that member published, or the tape becomes a window into other people's betting. Why: Players remember rounds, not totals, and the round rows exist but are never shown back to the person who played them. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Best-value game recommender

Details

From the member's own rounds, the per-game XP rules and the configured bet bounds, the hub computes where that member's XP per coin and observed net are best right now - 'Tower of Badges has returned 0.6 XP per coin over 214 rounds; roulette has returned 0.2' - and shows one suggestion. It recommends a game rather than a stake, which keeps it separate from the bet coach. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a recommender that confidently points at a house-edge game encourages chasing, so it must report observed XP per coin and state plainly that coin expectation is negative. Why: Thirteen games with different value profiles and no guidance is a puzzle the site can answer from data it already has. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Weekly game mutators

Details

Every week the panel picks one mutator that changes the rules of an existing game for everyone - double-or-nothing blackjack, slots that pay on two of a kind with a halved bet, a roulette green-zero bonus - announced on the hub with the affected game and reverted automatically at the week's end. A mutator is a small server-side override read from the settings document by the resolver the games already use, so it needs no new game code. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a mutator that improves expected value becomes a week-long farming route, so each one must be checked against the payout maths before it ships and the settings document must stay the only source so a bad one can be switched off mid-week. Why: The same 13 games every day is the arcade's biggest retention problem, and rule twists buy novelty without shipping new games. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Post-round skill diagnostic

Details

After a skill round that was lost, or that ended below a configured threshold, the result panel shows a short server-generated explanation of the decisive input - 'you called it at floor 6 with a 22 percent bust chance; cashing at floor 4 was the value line' - built from the recorded inputs and the same probability constants the engine used. It is generated for that round only, never stored as advice, and never shown for the luck games where no decision existed. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an explanation that is wrong or post-hoc destroys trust, so only inputs with a known optimal line should produce a diagnostic and the rest must say there was no decision to review. Why: Skill games currently return a score and nothing else, so a loss teaches a player nothing about the next round. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Cross-game parlay ticket

Details

A member builds one ticket of two to four legs across different games - each leg a stated condition such as 'win a round of Higher or Lower' or 'survive floor 3 of Tower' - staking a single amount and taking a combined multiplier derived from the legs' published odds, with every leg to be played inside a fixed window. The engine resolves each leg through the normal round path and holds the ticket until it is settled or expires. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: a parlay compounds the house edge and invites ruin-chasing, so the leg count, the stake cap and the expiry must be tight and the multiplier must be computed from the constants rather than hand-set. Why: The 13 games are currently isolated from each other, and a ticket is the simplest way to make them reference one another. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Cross-game handoff streak

Details

Winning a round unlocks a handoff: the next round played in a different game carries a small XP and scrip bonus, and the bonus grows as the chain moves through distinct games, resetting if the member repeats a game or loses. The chain state is per day, kept on the progress row, and drawn as a small counter in the arcade header. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: an escalating multiplier across games rewards speed over thought, so the bonus must cap at a low multiple and apply to XP and scrip only, never to payouts. Why: Nothing currently encourages a member to try a second game, and the cheapest fix is a reward for variety rather than a new game. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Quick-bet rail with bet memory

Details

A rail under the game controls offers halve, double, minimum, maximum and repeat-last-bet, with the last stake per game persisted on the progress row so returning to a game restores it. The rail reads the per-game bounds from the same settings the engine enforces, so it can never offer a stake the server would reject. Effort: S. Hold-to-confirm for large bets

Details

Any stake above a per-member threshold, set in arcade preferences and defaulting to a fraction of the balance, must be confirmed by holding the play control for a moment while the amount and the possible loss are shown; below the threshold nothing changes. The threshold and the resulting confirmation are flagged on the round. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a confirmation that can be switched off entirely removes the protection from the people it exists for, so the threshold may be raised but not disabled below a floor. Why: A single misclick at the maximum bet can erase a session, and the arcade currently offers no friction at all on the largest action. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Personal arcade day boundary

Details

The daily loop turns over at a fixed UTC time, which is invisible in the UI; this feature derives each member's own boundary from the timezone offset already stored with their analytics and shows 'your day ends in 3h - streak and spin still unclaimed' with a one-tap jump to whatever is outstanding. The boundary only changes what the interface displays and warns about; the server keeps its existing UTC gate so a day can never be claimed twice. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: if the local boundary ever feeds the claim gate it opens double-claiming across timezones, so display and enforcement must stay separate and the separation must be stated in the code. Why: Members lose streaks to a reset they cannot see, and the site already knows each visitor's timezone offset. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Streak-scaled wheel table

Details

The daily wheel's prize table upgrades with the member's login streak: at 7, 30 and 100 days the low tiers are removed or doubled, visible on the wheel as the segments that fall away and stated on the page. It reads the existing streak counter and wheel slots, and each spin records which table version it used. This differs from a pity timer because the improvement comes from returning, not from a run of poor spins. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a streak multiplier compounds with the daily bonus, so the upgrade must favour the XP-heavy tiers and leave coin prizes flat or long streaks become the site's best coin faucet. Why: The wheel pays the same on day 1 and day 100, so the returning player's reward is identical to a stranger's. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Wheel risk choice

Details

Before spinning, the member picks between two boards: the standard board with steady small prizes, or the long-shot board that is mostly nothing with occasional large XP and scrip, both published with their weights and both holding the same expected value. The chosen board is recorded with the spin, and the boards' composition rotates each season. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: two boards with identical expected value still feel rigged if only one is reported on, so the history page must show observed results for both boards or the choice only adds distrust. Why: The wheel is the same 30-second ritual every day, and a single choice before the spin gives it a decision. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Earned bonus wheel spins

Details

A member earns at most one extra spin per day by completing an arcade objective after the daily spin (win three rounds of any game), plus one more for a successful heist; extra spins use a reduced prize table and are stored as vouchers on the progress row so they cannot be claimed twice. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: extra spins on the full table multiply the daily faucet, so the reduced table and the hard cap of two extra spins a day are the whole safety mechanism. Why: The wheel is the arcade's strongest return hook and currently ends the moment the single spin is spent. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Crew heist raid

Details

Three to five members raid one target in a timed window: each pays the attempt cost, the engine resolves the raid as one combined heist whose chance scales with the participants' combined level difference against the target, and the take splits by contribution while the target's normal defence applies once. The victim sees a single raid notification rather than a burst of pings. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: a group raid multiplies exactly the pressure the per-victim flood check exists to limit, so the raid must count against every participant's hourly cap and against a global daily cap on the target. Why: Stealing is currently a lone act, and the crews and fleets the site already has give members a natural group to raid with. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Neutral

Heist bait and trace

Details

A member marks part of their balance as bait; if a heist against them succeeds that day the bait amount is flagged and the thief carries a visible 'traced' marker for 24 hours, during which their own heists succeed less often. The bait is disclosed to no one in advance, so it is a trap the victim sets, and the marker clears on the timer rather than by paying it off. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: an invisible defence that punishes thieves at random reads as unfair, so the trace must be announced to the thief when it fires with its reason and duration. Why: The steal settings let a member choose a price but give them no way to do anything active about being targeted. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Catalog Net-Flow Ledger

Details

A stacked-area time series of catalog change per ISO week: badges first seen (the first badge_events sighting, falling back to badges.created_at), badges that expired, and a net line for resulting catalog size. Each week shows gross inflow, gross outflow and the net, so the operator sees at a glance whether the catalog is growing, churning or shrinking. Reads badge_events joined to badges. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a re-sync that re-emits an unchanged badge as a 'new' event would inflate inflow, so first-seen must be deduplicated by badge id. Why: Turns the raw event log into the single-line answer to whether the catalog is healthy. Source: new-ideas-06-stats.json

Details

Consecutive wins within a day build a combo that multiplies game XP, for example a three-win run giving 1.5x, resetting on a loss or at the daily reset and computed server-side from the member's round outcomes. It appears as a small combo meter in the arcade and writes a feed entry when it breaks a personal record, while the daily game-XP cap still bounds the total. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it amplifies variance and rewards the highest-variance game, so the combo must be capped low and applied only to win XP, never to coins. Why: It adds a skill-flavoured reward loop to an arcade that currently pays every win the same. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Coins Neutral

Mid-Round Cash-Out

Details

In the games with a running multiplier, such as tower, the coin-flip ladder and higher-or-lower, the player gets a server-enforced settle-now action that banks the current value instead of continuing, recorded with the round outcome in game_rounds. It reads the same server-authoritative round state those games already keep. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a race between the settle call and the next round step lets a player bank after seeing the next result, so the decision must be resolved inside the single server round transition. Why: It turns pure nerve into a real decision in the risk games, which is what makes them replayable. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Sonstiges Neutral

Provider contract fixtures: record every payload shape and diff it on each sync

Details

Commit a small corpus of real recorded responses per provider (`badgebase` RSS + detail page, `potat` `/twitch/badges`, `perfil`, Helix/IVR catalog) and, at the start of every sync, compare the live response's key set and value types against the fixture. A mismatch (renamed field, array became object, null where a number was) is written as a changelog row with `kind = 'provider_drift'` and escalates to a push notification. The province here is not parsing — it is noticing that parsing silently returned `undefined` for a field that used to exist. Effort: M. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-09.md

Badges Aufwändig

Verifiable Badge Credential

Details

A user can export a signed, offline-verifiable attestation of their badge inventory (an Ed25519-signed payload with the badge IDs, issuance date and a verification URL) that any third party can check without calling the site. A public `/verify` page accepts pasted credentials and shows pass/fail with the underlying claims. Why it fits: The project already resolves true per-user badge ownership from badges.blog/GQL, which is the hard part; signing the resolved result adds the one missing primitive — a trust boundary that lets "I own this badge" travel outside the site. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: Signing without binding the credential to a login and a short validity window lets a stale credential assert a collection forever — the payload needs issuer, subject and expiry or the signature proves nothing. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Badges Aufwändig

Live Owner-Delta Stream

Details

Replace the once-a-minute `LiveRefresher` poll on badge pages with a server-sent-events stream that pushes owner-count deltas as they land from the sync, driving an animated counter and a rolling momentum sparkline. A "surge" badge appears live on the catalog grid for any badge whose delta crosses a threshold between polls. Why it fits: The `badge_momentum` view already computes the rate of change, and the 15-minute cadence means there is a continuous supply of deltas — the site currently throws that liveness away by polling the same number. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: A stream that is really a 60-second poll wearing an SSE costume is worse than the poll, and a hypothesis-free sparkline on a 15-minute data source implies a precision the data does not have. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-01.md

Spiele Aufwändig

Drop Window Rhythm

Details

Each round renders a track built from real badgebase start and end dates, and the player taps as a moving marker crosses each window's open and close edges. Accuracy is scored in server-measured milliseconds from the round timestamp rather than the tap's claimed time, with a perfect-chain multiplier that resets on an early or late hit. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: network latency decides perfect taps for distant players, so score against a server-issued clock offset and allow a fair tolerance band. Why: It makes the catalog's actual countdown data the rhythm track of a game. Source: new-ideas-04-game.json

`verify:deploy` — an automated post-deploy smoke check against production

Details

A script that, given the production URL, asserts response status and a content marker for a fixed short list: `/api/health`, the sitemap, one badge detail page in each of the 11 locales (to catch `MISSING_MESSAGE`, which the build does not fail on), and the RSS/changelog feed. This is not the CI `verify` script from IMPROVEMENTS.md §10 — it checks the *deployed artifact*, which is the only place a locale or RLS breakage becomes visible. Effort: S/M. Content Aufwändig

TBRI methodology and caveats page

Details

A public explanation of how the rarity index and its tiers are computed: the six signals, their weights, the tier cut-offs, what is genuinely unknowable (third-party owner estimates), and one fully worked example badge showing every intermediate number. It imports the same constants module the rarity code uses and computes the worked example live from a real badge row, so the published formula cannot silently diverge from the engine. It also states the refresh cadence and the known edge cases (badges with no owner data, paid versus free windows). Data: the rarity constants, one live badge row, that badge's badge_stats series. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: the page describes an older formula after a scoring change and the site's own transparency artifact becomes misinformation; derive displayed numbers from the live function instead of restating them. Why: The rarity index is the site's core claim to authority, and a methodology page is what separates a computed number from a made-up one. Source: new-ideas-02-content.json

Add-ons Aufwändig

Mistake drill from your misses

Details

Quiz, memory and vault already know which question or tile a member got wrong, so a drill mode serves a short no-wager round built only from that member's recent misses, running the same engine and the same one-round-per-second limit. An item clears once answered correctly twice, and drill results pay only a small daily-capped XP trickle instead of coins. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a drill that pays like a real round is free money, so the XP must be capped per day and per drill and the drill set must be rebuilt from the misses rather than hoarded. Why: The skill games currently punish the same knowledge gap forever, and the misses are already recorded. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Aufwändig

Round review marketplace

Details

A member requests a paid review of their last N rounds in one game, optionally naming a higher-mastery reviewer; the requester escrows coins, the reviewer writes a short structured critique of bet sizing, cash-out timing and mistake pattern from the round tape, and payment releases on submission with a dispute window. Reviewer reputation comes from reviews written and ratings received, and the work is a one-off audit rather than the ongoing pairing the mentorship idea describes. Effort: L. Risk if built badly: this is a marketplace for advice, so escrow, dispute handling and a volume cap are required, and a reviewer must never see rounds the requester did not explicitly share. Why: Mastery tracks will create exactly the kind of experts whose knowledge is worth paying for, and the round tape gives them something to read. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Add-ons Aufwändig

Press-your-luck daily claim

Details

The daily login bonus becomes a choice: take today's amount, or defer it and double it tomorrow - but deferring loses the day if tomorrow's claim is missed, and only the most recent day can ever be deferred. The decision is stored with the claim date and the doubling is shown before the choice is made, so the gamble is explicit. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a doubling that can be stacked becomes an exponential faucet, so only one deferred day may exist at a time and a late claim must decay rather than pay. Why: The login bonus is a button that everyone presses without thinking, and a single decision makes the daily ritual a choice. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Details

Reads system_heartbeats. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: normal retry jitter looks like irregularity, so a per-source tolerance band is needed and the raw intervals must remain visible. Why: Catches degrading sync schedules before they become outright outages that freshness monitoring only notices late. Source: new-ideas-06-stats.json

Coins Aufwändig

Self-Escrow Commitment Contracts

Details

A member escrows coins against a self-set goal for the day or week, such as winning five arcade rounds or claiming the next global drop, held by the server and released back with a bonus if the goal verifies, or burned if it fails. Verification reads the existing game_rounds and inventory sync data, and escrow is one-way and capped so it cannot be used to shield coins from stealing. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a goal that cannot be verified honestly becomes a coin-laundering path, so only server-checkable goals may be offered. Why: It turns the coin economy into a personal-motivation tool, which is the one thing coins can do that XP cannot. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Coins Aufwändig

Savings Vault with Interest

Details

A member can lock a chosen number of coins for a fixed term such as 7 or 30 days; the deposit earns interest funded from the arcade's house edge and is released at term end, and cannot be withdrawn or stolen while locked. It is the deliberate opposite of the anti-hoard tax, rewarding saving only when coins are genuinely committed. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it becomes a free inflation machine, so the rate must sit below the house edge and the terms must be long enough that deposits cannot be cycled. Why: It gives coin holders a reason to plan instead of gambling everything at once, and gives the economy its first long-term sink. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Details

A member who hits near-zero coins receives a small one-time recovery grant, once per cooldown period, so one bad session does not lock them out of the arcade entirely. It is granted server-side and recorded in both the changelog and the economy ledger. Effort: S. After a successful heist the victim can post a public bounty in coins on the thief, and any other member who successfully steals from that thief and claims it splits the bounty. Coins Aufwändig

Parlay Betting Slip

Details

A member builds a slip of two to five picks across different arcade games and stakes once; every pick must win for a multiplied payout and any loss voids the slip. The server resolves the slip from the same per-game outcomes the individual rounds already produce, so no new randomness is introduced. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a lucky parlay creates a coin windfall that wrecks the economy, so the multiplier must be capped and the slip stake limited well below the normal maximum bet. Why: It adds a genuinely new wagering shape to an arcade whose games are all single-round today. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Coins Aufwändig

Self-Set Loss Limits

Details

A member can set a personal coin-loss limit per day or per session; once it is hit the arcade locks their betting until the period resets, with removal possible only after a cooling-off delay rather than a fee. It is opt-in, stored with their other settings, and displayed like a budget. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: it becomes nagware members resent, so it must never be applied silently and must be easy to start, hard to switch off on impulse. Why: A real coin economy on a gambling-flavoured arcade owes its players a way to stop. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Sonstiges Aufwändig

Boundary validation for provider payloads (parse, don't assume)

Details

Every provider payload crosses one schema guard before any row is built; a payload that fails validation aborts that provider's write and records a heartbeat with the failing field path and the raw sample. Fields that are genuinely optional are marked optional in the schema rather than being coerced with `?? null` at the call site, so "missing" and "empty" stop being the same thing. This is the *validate after sync* counterpart to a dry run: the sync either wrote validated data or wrote nothing and said why. Effort: M. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-09.md

XP Aufwändig

Stake-Weighted Game XP

Details

Replace the flat +10 win and +2 loss with an XP amount scaled by the stake bracket of the round, for example 5/10/20/40 XP by bet size, still bounded by the existing daily game-XP cap. The value is computed server-side in round resolution from the bet actually placed against that game's minBet and maxBet settings. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: it rewards betting large over playing well and can be gamed with maximum-bet coin flips, so the scaling must stay sub-linear in the stake and the daily cap must hold. Why: It links XP to how members actually choose to play instead of treating every round as identical. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Add-ons Aufwändig

Spectator channel for public rounds

Details

A member can switch 'broadcast my rounds' on for a session; the arcade then publishes each resolved round as a lightweight activity event whose id appears on a /watch page that polls or subscribes over SSE for the newest rounds of members currently broadcasting. Watchers see the game, the bet tier, win or loss and the payout, with the player's identity shown only if they opted into being named. This differs from a shared multiplayer table (where the watcher also plays) and from the site-wide activity feed (which includes everything). Effort: M. Why: The arcade has no audience layer at all, and the big wins that make the feed are exactly the moments nobody can currently watch live. Source: new-ideas-05-addon.json

Coins Aufwändig

Session Loss Rebate

Details

At the end of a losing session, defined as a contiguous run of play, the house returns a small percentage of net losses as a rebate capped at a low daily ceiling, computed from game_rounds and paid through the normal award path. Unlike insurance it is automatic and costs nothing to receive. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: rebates raise the effective return-to-player enough to inflate the economy, so the percentage must be tiny and the daily cap enforced. Why: It softens the arcade's worst nights without changing how a normal one feels. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Sonstiges Aufwändig

Write-band assertion: a sync that writes an implausible number of rows must shout

Details

After each engine finishes, compare rows written/deleted per table against a rolling baseline (previous runs' median plus a tolerance band) and classify the run as `ok`, `suspicious`, or `aborted`. Anything outside the band becomes a changelog row plus a push, naming the table and the delta; the run still commits, so nothing is lost, but a sweep that deleted 100% of the catalog can no longer look like a normal nightly. Effort: S. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-09.md

XP Aufwändig

Level-Race Weeks

Details

Two members opt into a seven-day head-to-head XP race; the server snapshots both XP totals at accept time and renders a live comparison bar from the delta, read from timestamped activity_events for the two ids. The winner gets a temporary Racer flair and a modest XP bonus, and an inactive or tied week resolves as a draw. Entry is free and one active race per member. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a player parks earning and dumps it in the final hour, so the race must be scored from timestamped activity deltas and must not create a second board that often sits empty. Why: It makes the XP ladder personal and social for the members who will never crack the global top 100. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Branching Level-Up Perks

Details

On each level-up the member picks one of two timed perks for the next stretch, for example +20 percent game XP for 24 hours versus +5 percentage points of steal success for 24 hours, chosen in the level-up modal and stored on user_progress with an expiry. The perks act through the same server-side award and heist paths that read the economy constants, so they are real effects rather than client-side claims. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: stacking perks across levels compounds into an exploit, so only one may be active at a time and every multiplier must be applied inside the server award. Why: It turns a passive level number into a decision the member actually makes, which is what a levels system is for. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Details

At level 100 a member may Ascend: the level counter returns to 1, the curve restarts under a small permanent global multiplier capped at a few ascensions, and each ascension adds a permanent star to the level badge. Lifetime XP and a new ascensions counter are kept, so nothing is erased, and it is explicitly not the seasonal game-ladder reset. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: the multiplier compounds across many ascensions into runaway XP, so the bonus must be hard-capped and the curve retuned so ascending is never worse than idling at the cap. Details

Admin Aufwändig

Details

Every settings write already lands in the audit log, and this adds a one-click revert that restores the previous value of a single settings key from the recorded snapshot with a diff before confirming. It targets the site_settings economy, feature and maintenance documents and reads the audit log rather than a new history table. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: reverting one key silently reverts others or resurrects a value the current schema no longer holds, so the revert must be per-key and re-validated. Admin Aufwändig

Details

A feature such as a game, the wheel or steals can carry an automatic trip condition, an error rate or economy anomaly above a threshold, that flips it off and writes a notice to the panel with no operator present. It reads the existing heartbeat and game_rounds error signals and uses the same feature-flag write path as the manual switches. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a transient spike kills a game for everyone mid-event, so tripping must require a sustained breach, show a reason, and be trivially reversible. Why: One broken game should not be able to burn the economy all night while the operator sleeps. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Admin Aufwändig

Incident Playbooks on Alerts

Details

Each panel alert, such as a failed sync, a heartbeats-gone-quiet condition or an economy anomaly, can carry a short operator playbook listing the checks to run and the safe action to take, rendered beside the alert and editable in the panel. It reads the alert and status data the Status tab already computes. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: a stale playbook sends an operator down a wrong path, so each playbook needs a last-reviewed date and version history. Why: The panel tells an operator something is wrong but never what to do about it, and that difference matters at 03:00. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Admin Aufwändig

Dual-Control Approval for Destructive Actions

Details

Destructive operations such as purge, bulk delete, maintenance on or an economy reset require a second admin to approve within a short window before executing, with both the request and the approval written to the audit log. It complements per-action permissions by making the most dangerous actions two-person rather than one-person. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: a single-admin team is locked out during an emergency, so a documented break-glass path with its own loud audit entry must exist. Why: The panel lets one person delete the catalog, and nothing today makes a second person look first. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Admin Aufwändig

Idea Deduplication Assistant

Details

When an idea is submitted to the brainstorm board, the panel shows the closest existing ideas by text similarity and category so an operator can merge or reject a duplicate before it lands, the exact costly failure mode this site keeps hitting. It reads the brainstorm ideas table and is purely advisory, never blocking a submission. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: it hides a genuinely new idea behind a false match, so similarity must never become an automatic rejection. Why: The idea board's worst outcome is the duplicate nobody noticed, and this catches it at entry. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Admin Aufwändig

Settings Drift Snapshot and Diff

Details

The panel periodically snapshots the effective site_settings documents and diffs them against the code defaults, flagging keys changed from the constant the code falls back to and any key present in code but missing in the database. It reads the economy and game accessors against the settings table. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: it cries wolf on intentional tuning, so it must separate a deliberate audited change from an unnoticed drift. Why: The economy silently depends on settings that fall back to code constants, and drift between the two is invisible until it breaks. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Admin Aufwändig

Step-Up Re-authentication for Sensitive Tabs

Details

Opening the settings, sync or audit tabs after a period of inactivity demands a fresh Twitch confirmation before the panel will act, refreshing the session rather than adding a second factor. It reads the existing session and role and wraps the sensitive API routes. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: it locks an operator out mid-incident, so the challenge must be short-lived and re-entry quick and auditable. Why: An unattended open panel with a service-role client behind it is the site's largest single risk, and this shrinks the window cheaply. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Admin Aufwändig

Infrastructure Quota and Cost Monitor

Details

A panel tab tracks the platform limits this project actually lives inside, covering Vercel Hobby function invocations and cron minutes, Supabase database size and egress, and the 60-second function budget, with a warning line before a limit is hit. It reads provider usage where exposed plus the sync heartbeats' recorded durations. Effort: M. Risk if built badly: provider APIs may change or not be exposed on Hobby, so the tab must degrade to honest in-app counters rather than pretend to know the provider's numbers. Why: This site runs on a free tier with hard walls, and hitting one silently takes the whole thing down. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

Sonstiges Aufwändig

Per-source freshness SLO, exposed both to `/stats` and as curl-able JSON

Details

Define a maximum age per data source (catalog, drop windows, owner stats, per-user inventory) and derive a per-source status from the newest row each engine wrote — not from "did the cron tick". `/stats` shows the four staleness gauges next to the existing uptime meter, and `/api/health` returns the same structure as JSON so an external monitor or a GitHub Actions step can assert it. A source that is *ticking but stale* is the exact failure a heartbeat cannot see. Effort: S. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-09.md

Sonstiges Aufwändig

Sync cost and time budget guard (serverless wall clock + external call volume)

Details

Instrument each engine with its wall-clock time and its outbound call count, and assert both against an explicit budget derived from the platform limits (60 s per serverless invocation; a polite call ceiling for the ToS-sensitive providers). When a run approaches the budget it stops taking new work, commits what it has, and records a `partial` heartbeat instead of being killed mid-flight. The numbers are also the honest answer to "what does this cost on Hobby". Effort: M. Source: bugreports/agent-idea-09.md

XP Aufwändig

Discovery XP for First-Viewed Badges

Details

Reading a badge detail page the member has never viewed grants a small one-time XP such as +5, recorded per user and badge UUID so it can never pay twice, with a daily ceiling around 50 XP. It reads the existing page views and the badge catalog and gives signed-in readers a reason to explore the 476-badge archive rather than only the drop of the day. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: crawlers and refresh loops farm it, so it must be gated to a signed-in member, permanently deduplicated per badge, and capped per day. Why: The catalog is the site's whole asset, and this pays members for actually reading it. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json

XP Aufwändig

Anti-Tilt Residual XP

Details

A member on a losing streak such as five consecutive losses in a day earns a small rising consolation XP on each further loss, so an unlucky session still moves the level bar, resetting on a win or at the daily reset and bounded by the game-XP cap. It is computed server-side from the streak of round outcomes. Effort: S. Risk if built badly: it pays members to lose deliberately, so the consolation must be worth far less than a win and must stop at a small daily ceiling. Why: It keeps a bad-luck evening from feeling like the site wasted the member's time. Source: new-ideas-10-xp-coin-admin.json
